ABPMR Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Check serum ferritin, replace iron if low, and review the contribution of her antidepressant): Evening urge to move, relief with walking, and sleep delay indicate restless legs syndrome, which is more frequent in multiple sclerosis. Low iron stores and serotonergic antidepressants both worsen it. Current practice favors iron repletion when ferritin is low and drug review before dopamine agonists, which risk augmentation.
  2. The correct answer is A (Axial spondyloarthritis, with sacroiliac MRI showing bone marrow edema before radiographs become abnormal): Young age, insidious onset, prolonged morning stiffness, improvement with exercise, night pain in the second half of the night and buttock pain describe inflammatory back pain from axial spondyloarthritis. MRI shows sacroiliac bone marrow edema years before radiographs show sacroiliitis. Degenerative disc and facet disease and DISH are typical of older patients.
  3. The correct answer is D (An in-plane approach, keeping the entire shaft and tip in the beam throughout advancement): An in-plane technique shows the full needle shaft and tip, which is the safest way to avoid the adjacent artery when the target is deep. Out-of-plane imaging shows a dot that may be a shaft cross-section rather than the tip. Steep angles reflect the beam away from the probe, and landmark guidance gives up the advantage of imaging.
  4. The correct answer is A (Reposition the limb until the muscle relaxes, then sample several sites for regular potentials): Spontaneous potentials must be assessed with the muscle at rest, so the practical step is to reposition the limb, use distraction or reassurance, and sample several sites to see regular, unchanging potentials. Changing filters distorts waveforms, contraction hides spontaneous activity, and insertional activity alone is a poor substitute.
  5. The correct answer is D (Neuralgic amyotrophy (brachial neuritis) with patchy multifocal involvement of upper plexus branches): Acute severe shoulder pain that subsides as weakness appears, after a viral illness, is typical of Parsonage Turner syndrome, which often involves the suprascapular, axillary and long thoracic nerves. Full passive range and a winged scapula do not fit a cuff tear. Normal neck testing and imaging weaken the case for radiculopathy.
  6. The correct answer is C (Offload the area until the erythema resolves and revise her pressure relief schedule): Nonblanchable erythema with intact skin is a stage 1 pressure injury, and the key treatment is removing pressure from the site and correcting the cause. Her relief schedule and seating should be reviewed. Dressings alone do not stop ongoing pressure, and massage can injure compromised tissue.
  7. The correct answer is A (Extrusion, a herniation whose outer portion is wider than its base at the disc margin): In the combined task force nomenclature, extrusion means disc material beyond the disc space with a dimension in at least one plane greater than the base at the disc margin. Protrusion has a base wider than any extended portion, and a bulge is a broad symmetric extension. Sequestration requires loss of continuity with the parent disc, which the description does not state.
  8. The correct answer is D (Low-load prolonged stretching with dynamic splinting plus quadriceps strengthening to restore knee extension): In juvenile arthritis with controlled inflammation, a flexion contracture is best treated with gentle low-load prolonged stretch, serial or dynamic splinting, and strengthening of the antagonist muscle. Immobilization is tempting to protect the joint but increases stiffness and weakness. Manipulation and surgery are reserved for failure of conservative management.
  9. The correct answer is C (About 1.25 to 1.5 g/kg/day, because healing tissue raises protein needs above maintenance): Pressure injury guidelines recommend roughly 1.25 to 1.5 g/kg/day of protein for patients with healing wounds who are malnourished or at risk, provided renal function allows. The maintenance level of 0.8 g/kg/day under-supplies a catabolic wound. Serum albumin reflects inflammation more than nutrition and should not drive restriction.
  10. The correct answer is A (Offload with a total contact cast or boot, and add wound care and glycemic review): A neuropathic plantar ulcer without infection or bone involvement heals mainly through pressure offloading, with wound care and glucose control. Total contact casting or an offloading boot is the standard approach, and mobility with the prosthesis can be managed with assistive support. Antibiotics and MRI are not indicated without infection signs, and surgery comes after failed conservative care.
  11. The correct answer is C (Demonstrated eccentric strength and neuromuscular control to land and squat with aligned lower limb mechanics): Plyometric loading demands high eccentric force absorption and good landing control, so readiness is judged by strength and movement quality, and dynamic valgus on single-leg tasks signals higher risk of reinjury. Time since surgery, range of motion and isolated strength symmetry are necessary but do not show she can control the limb under impact.
  12. The correct answer is A (Stage 3, because full-thickness loss is present while fascia, muscle, tendon and bone remain covered): Visible subcutaneous fat with granulation tissue and no exposed deeper structures defines a stage 3 pressure injury. A stage 4 injury requires exposed fascia, muscle, tendon, ligament, cartilage, or bone. Unstageable applies only when slough or eschar obscures the base, which is not the case here.
  13. The correct answer is B (Rapid bone loss with rebound vertebral fractures within months, so a follow-on agent is usually needed): Denosumab effect wears off quickly after the last dose, turnover surges above baseline, and multiple vertebral fractures have been reported within months. A bisphosphonate is typically given as a transition. Bisphosphonates, by contrast, remain bound to bone and fade slowly.
  14. The correct answer is B (An ACE inhibitor plus a beta blocker, titrated carefully as tolerated, managed jointly with cardiology): Duchenne cardiomyopathy is progressive, and symptomatic left ventricular dysfunction should receive standard heart failure therapy, including an ACE inhibitor and a beta blocker, with cardiology input. Tall right precordial R waves are a typical baseline finding and do not change management. Waiting for a lower ejection fraction or using diuretics alone leaves disease modifying therapy unused.
  15. The correct answer is C (Topical lidocaine patches over the painful dermatome, with minimal systemic absorption and little sedation): For localized postherpetic neuralgia, topical lidocaine is a first-line option and suits an older adult at risk of falls and cognitive effects. Tricyclics are effective, but their anticholinergic activity worsens urinary retention and cognition. Gabapentin needs low starting doses with slow titration, and opioids are not first-line.
  16. The correct answer is D (Resume baclofen at the prior dose promptly and observe closely for seizures and autonomic instability): Abrupt baclofen cessation causes a withdrawal syndrome with hallucinations, seizures, and rebound spasticity, and the treatment is to restore baclofen quickly. Haloperidol can worsen seizure risk, and phenytoin or dantrolene do not reverse the underlying GABA-B deficiency. Supervised observation is needed.
  17. The correct answer is A (Short-term relief is common, but intermediate and long-term outcomes are worse than with therapy): Trials of lateral epicondylalgia show early pain relief after corticosteroid injection but poorer outcomes and more recurrence at intermediate and one year follow-up than with physical therapy. The condition is a degenerative tendinopathy rather than inflammation. Repeated injections add risk without proven durable benefit.
  18. The correct answer is B (Mature bone forms at the periphery with immature osteoid at the center, a zonal pattern): Myositis ossificans matures from the outside in, so mature lamellar bone rims the lesion and immature tissue sits centrally, producing the ring-like radiographic appearance. Osteosarcoma shows the opposite pattern, with the most immature, disorganized tissue peripherally. A Codman triangle suggests an aggressive tumor.
  19. The correct answer is C (Posterior basal segments of the lower lobes, with the right side favored by bronchial anatomy): In an upright patient, aspirated material falls into the dependent posterior basal segments of the lower lobes, and the right main bronchus is wider and straighter, so the right side is favored. The posterior upper lobe segment is the classic site after aspiration while supine, which does not fit this seated mealtime event.
  20. The correct answer is A (Symptomatic neuroma of a transected cutaneous nerve, producing a positive Tinel sign at the pressure point): A neuroma forms at the end of a transected nerve and gives focal, electric pain with a positive Tinel sign when pressed or tapped. Phantom limb pain is felt in the absent limb, not at a tapped spot on the stump. Normal radiographs argue against a bone spur or heterotopic ossification.
  21. The correct answer is C (Oral fidaxomicin or oral vancomycin, plus contact precautions and stopping the inciting antibiotic if possible): Her white cell count of 15,000 or more marks severe disease, and current guidelines favor oral fidaxomicin or vancomycin over metronidazole, which is now reserved for limited circumstances. Antimotility agents risk toxic megacolon. Intravenous vancomycin does not reach the colonic lumen in adequate concentration.
  22. The correct answer is A (Early Syme or Boyd ankle disarticulation, followed by a prosthesis with an extension for the discrepancy): With a severe projected discrepancy, an unstable ankle and a nonfunctional foot, early Syme or Boyd amputation gives an end-bearing limb that fits a prosthesis around the age of walking. Lengthening carries high complication rates in this setting and rarely salvages a useful foot. Rotationplasty belongs to proximal femoral focal deficiency, not fibular hemimelia.
  23. The correct answer is C (Early surgical repair of the distal biceps tendon to the radial tuberosity within a few weeks): A complete distal biceps rupture in an active young or middle-aged manual worker is repaired early, since delay makes repair harder and nonoperative care leaves lasting loss of supination and flexion strength. The brachialis can substitute for flexion but not for supination.
  24. The correct answer is C (Perform a comparative median versus ulnar sensory study to the ring finger or palm): Mild carpal tunnel syndrome can have normal standard latencies, and comparative studies within the same hand are more sensitive because they cancel out temperature and systemic factors. A median versus ulnar ring finger or palmar comparison is the usual next step. Stopping at normal standard studies would miss early disease.
  25. The correct answer is A (Anemia of inflammation, with hepcidin-mediated iron sequestration in macrophages limiting release to erythroid precursors): Low iron with low binding capacity and high ferritin is the signature of anemia of inflammation, driven by hepcidin trapping iron. Iron deficiency raises binding capacity and lowers ferritin, although it can coexist with inflammation. Kidney disease anemia reflects low erythropoietin and is not suggested by this scenario.
  26. The correct answer is C (Progressive loading with eccentric and heavy slow resistance calf exercises over at least twelve weeks): Midportion Achilles tendinopathy is a degenerative loading problem, and progressive eccentric or heavy slow resistance exercise over about twelve weeks has the strongest evidence. Rest and boots deload without remodeling the tendon. Repeated steroid injections around the tendon increase rupture risk, and stretching alone does not provide the needed loading.
  27. The correct answer is A (Three phase bone scan, which detects increased uptake before bone appears on plain radiographs): Neurogenic heterotopic ossification in the first weeks after spinal cord injury presents with warmth, swelling, fever, and loss of range, and the three phase bone scan becomes positive before radiographs. Plain films lag by weeks, and the negative ultrasound already makes thrombosis unlikely as the explanation.
  28. The correct answer is B (Start a neuropathic pain agent such as gabapentin and reassure her that dysesthesia usually resolves): Painful dysesthesia is a recognized complication of phenol on a mixed nerve because sensory fibers are also injured, and it generally improves over weeks to months. Neuropathic pain medication and reassurance are appropriate while the spasticity benefit is preserved. Repeating the injection would add more sensory injury.
  29. The correct answer is B (Achilles lengthening combined with split anterior tibialis tendon transfer to the lateral midfoot): The fixed equinus needs lengthening, and the continuous tibialis anterior activity in stance drives the varus, so a split transfer to the lateral midfoot rebalances the foot. Lengthening alone leaves the deforming force in place. A whole tendon transfer risks overcorrection into valgus, and arthrodesis is reserved for rigid or failed cases.
  30. The correct answer is D (Stop donepezil because its vagotonic cardiac and gastrointestinal effects likely explain these problems): Cholinesterase inhibitors increase vagal tone and cause bradycardia, syncope, nausea, and weight loss, so stopping the drug is the safest step for this patient. Switching to another agent in the same class carries the same risk, and anticholinergics worsen cognition and counteract the treatment.
  31. The correct answer is A (Withhold stimulation to the affected leg until anticoagulation is established, because contraction may dislodge thrombus): Muscle contraction in a limb with an acute untreated deep vein thrombosis could dislodge the clot, so stimulation to that limb is withheld until it is treated. Low intensity can still cause contraction, and the risk is not confined to the calf muscles. Changing waveform type does not remove the risk.
  32. The correct answer is C (Extra depth wide toe box shoes with a soft molded insole and retrocapital metatarsal pad): Subluxed MTP joints with fat pad loss need room for deformity, cushioning, and a pad just proximal to the metatarsal heads to unload them. A rigid shank in narrow shoes crowds the toes, and a heel lift increases forefoot pressure.
  33. The correct answer is B (Digital rectal examination followed by gentle manual disimpaction, then resumption of the scheduled bowel program): Liquid stool leaking around retained stool in a patient with a neurogenic bowel suggests fecal impaction with overflow. Examination and manual disimpaction treat both diagnosis and cause. The benign abdomen makes urgent CT unnecessary, and more oral laxative or a large enema adds volume behind an impaction.
  34. The correct answer is D (Ankle-brachial index with exercise testing, to evaluate for arterial disease as the cause): Cramping that stops with standing alone, together with diminished pedal pulses, trophic skin changes and diabetes, points to vascular claudication. The mild stenosis on MRI is likely incidental. Ankle-brachial index with exercise testing evaluates the arterial cause, and more spinal or electrodiagnostic testing would not.
  35. The correct answer is D (A low-frequency curvilinear transducer, accepting lower resolution to gain penetration at depth): Sound penetration falls as frequency rises, so a joint seven centimeters deep needs a low-frequency, usually curvilinear, probe even though resolution is lower. High-frequency linear and hockey-stick probes suit superficial tendons and small joints, and a high-frequency curvilinear probe does not escape the penetration trade-off.
  36. The correct answer is C (Internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve, itself a branch of the vagus nerve): The internal branch of the superior laryngeal nerve, a vagal branch, carries sensation from the supraglottic larynx and triggers the protective cough. The external branch is motor to the cricothyroid, the recurrent nerve senses the subglottic region, and the glossopharyngeal nerve supplies the oropharynx and the gag reflex.
  37. The correct answer is B (Add scheduled basal insulin with nutritional coverage matched to the feeding schedule, plus correction doses): Sliding-scale insulin alone is discouraged because it chases hyperglycemia after the fact. Continuous feeds need basal insulin plus nutritional coverage that is held or reduced if feeds are interrupted, with correction doses added. Infusions belong in the intensive care setting, and oral sulfonylureas carry hypoglycemia risk with variable feeding.
  38. The correct answer is A (A total contact cast, because it cannot be removed and enforces consistent plantar pressure reduction): The ulcer is noninfected and perfusion is adequate, so a total contact cast is the gold standard offloading method. Its main advantage here is that the patient cannot remove it, which solves the adherence failure with the removable walker. Other removable devices and crutch restrictions depend on behavior that has already proven unreliable.
  39. The correct answer is A (Fibrillation potentials in the deltoid and teres minor, with normal triceps, infraspinatus, and supraspinatus): The axillary nerve supplies both deltoid and teres minor, and an isolated lesion spares muscles supplied by the suprascapular and radial nerves. Involvement of triceps would implicate the radial nerve and thus the posterior cord, while supraspinatus or infraspinatus changes would point to the upper trunk or suprascapular nerve. Sparing teres minor would also be inconsistent with an axillary lesion proximal to its branch.
  40. The correct answer is C (Serial casting at the end of available extension, with the cast changed every few days): A contracture that does not change under sedation is fixed, so a tone reducing injection will not restore range, and serial casting using low load prolonged stretch is the usual next step before surgery. Surgical lengthening is reserved for contractures that fail casting, and strengthening the opposing muscle cannot lengthen shortened tissue.
  41. The correct answer is C (C7, since elbow extensors grade three and the myotome above them is fully strong): The motor level is the lowest key muscle with a grade of at least 3 provided the key muscle above is 5. Here the triceps is 3 and the C6 wrist extensors are 5, so the level is C7. Finger flexors at 1 do not qualify, and the sensory level is defined separately.
  42. The correct answer is D (Modified Tardieu Scale, which records the angle of catch at fast stretch and full range): The Tardieu scale uses a slow stretch to find passive range and a fast stretch to find the angle of catch, so the difference reflects the dynamic spastic component. The Modified Ashworth grades resistance at a single speed and cannot separate contracture. The Penn scale and the GMFCS measure different constructs.
  43. The correct answer is B (They cover more area and allow drainage but heal with a pebbled, less cosmetic appearance): Meshing expands a limited donor area and lets blood and fluid escape, but the healed interstices leave a pebbled look. Sheet grafts give the best appearance and are favored on the face and hands. Meshed grafts are split thickness, so sweating and hair do not return, and they still need immobilization.
  44. The correct answer is D (Support the limb with a board keeping the knee extended, and encourage prone lying daily): Flexed positioning in a wheelchair or in bed promotes knee and hip flexion contracture, which can prevent prosthetic use. A residual limb support board keeps the knee extended when seated, and prone lying stretches the hip flexors. Pillows under the knee and hamstring-focused strengthening make the problem worse.
  45. The correct answer is B (Admit for monitoring of swallowing and breathing, since distant toxin spread is managed supportively): Generalized weakness, ptosis, a weak cry, and dysphagia days after injection suggest distant spread of toxin, which can compromise swallowing and breathing. Treatment is supportive with close observation because the blockade lasts until nerve terminals sprout. Pyridostigmine and immunoglobulin do not address the presynaptic toxin effect.
  46. The correct answer is D (Graded upright positioning using an abdominal binder, compression stockings, and liberalized oral fluids first): A rise in heart rate with a drop in pressure after weeks in bed is hypovolemic and deconditioning-related orthostatic intolerance. The first steps are graded upright exposure, abdominal binding, compression, and fluid liberalization. Medications are second line, and more bed rest or deferred testing prolongs the problem.
  47. The correct answer is B (Reduced central activation from an upper motor neuron lesion, with a healthy peripheral motor unit): With a fresh hemispheric stroke, hyperreflexia, normal unit morphology, and slow firing with poor recruitment, the lesion is central: motor neurons are not driven well. Peripheral lesions with block or axon loss reduce recruitment but the units that remain fire rapidly. Critical illness myopathy would show small short polyphasic units.
  48. The correct answer is C (High common mode rejection ratio, which cancels signals common to both inputs and amplifies differences): A differential amplifier subtracts the two inputs, so noise common to both is canceled and the difference between them is amplified. The common mode rejection ratio measures how well it does this. A notch filter distorts the waveform, and more gain amplifies noise as well as signal.
  49. The correct answer is A (Sertraline, a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor with a favorable seizure and cognitive side-effect profile): Sertraline is a well-tolerated initial agent for depression after brain injury and carries a low seizure and cognitive burden. Bupropion lowers the seizure threshold, which matters in a patient who has already had a posttraumatic seizure. Tricyclics and paroxetine add anticholinergic effects that can impair cognition.
  50. The correct answer is D (Obtain hip imaging and inflammatory markers to look for heterotopic ossification as a pain source): New agitation after a calm period, with a warm, stiff hip and low grade temperature after brain injury, suggests heterotopic ossification causing pain. Imaging and inflammatory markers should come before any medication change, because untreated pain drives behavior. Sedation or propranolol would hide the cause, and joint aspiration is not the first step with a low grade fever.
  51. The correct answer is D (Small short polyphasic motor units with early recruitment, and possible irritability in proximal muscles): A boy with calf pseudohypertrophy, Gowers maneuver and very high creatine kinase has a dystrophinopathy. Normal nerve conduction with small, short, polyphasic motor units and early recruitment is typical of a myopathy, and fibrillation potentials may appear in dystrophic muscle. Large long units are neurogenic and myotonia indicates a channelopathy.
  52. The correct answer is A (Refer to a driver rehabilitation specialist for a comprehensive clinical and on-road evaluation): Office screening tests and bedside examination do not reliably predict driving ability after stroke, especially with subtle attention deficits. A driver rehabilitation specialist performs clinical testing and an on-road assessment and can recommend adaptive equipment or training. Blanket clearance or prohibition, or informal supervision, bypass this evidence based process.
  53. The correct answer is C (An accessory deep peroneal nerve supplying the muscle, confirmed by stimulating behind the lateral malleolus): An accessory deep peroneal nerve runs behind the lateral malleolus and supplies part of the extensor digitorum brevis, so ankle stimulation misses those fibers and the response is smaller than with proximal stimulation. Stimulating behind the lateral malleolus produces a response and confirms the variant. Normal latency and a normal needle examination argue against a pathologic process.
  54. The correct answer is D (Surgical lengthening of the gastrocnemius or Achilles tendon after failed nonoperative measures): A fixed equinus that has failed casting and toxin is structural, so surgical lengthening of the gastrocnemius or Achilles tendon is the logical next step. Toxin lowers dynamic tone but does not lengthen fixed tissue, and an orthosis holds position rather than remodeling shortened tissue.
  55. The correct answer is A (Total surface bearing socket with a gel liner, which spreads load evenly over the whole limb): Total surface bearing designs with a gel or silicone liner distribute load over the whole limb and lower peak pressures, which suits thin tissue and neuropathy. Deepening the PTB bar loads the same tissue that is ulcerating. Supracondylar suspension changes anchoring rather than limb loading, and a rigid unlined socket raises shear.
  56. The correct answer is C (Increased delayed-phase periarticular uptake in the affected limb, which supports the diagnosis but is not required): Three-phase bone scan in CRPS typically shows diffuse increased periarticular uptake in the delayed phase of the affected limb. The study has useful negative predictive value but the diagnosis remains clinical. Focal cortical or single joint uptake points to other conditions.
  57. The correct answer is A (Obtain spinal MRI and urodynamics, then refer to neurosurgery for untethering if cord tethering is confirmed): New back pain, spasticity, and bladder change during growth in a repaired myelomeningocele indicate a tethered spinal cord. MRI and urodynamics support the diagnosis, and surgical untethering can stop progression. Medication or bracing would miss the cause, and the shunt has been checked.
  58. The correct answer is A (Broca aphasia, with nonfluent output, impaired repetition, and relatively preserved comprehension): Effortful agrammatic speech with good comprehension and impaired repetition defines Broca aphasia from a lesion in the left inferior frontal region. Transcortical motor aphasia looks similar but repetition is spared. Conduction aphasia has fluent speech, and global aphasia would impair comprehension.
  59. The correct answer is C (Three phase bone scan, which shows increased uptake weeks before plain radiographs become abnormal): Three phase bone scintigraphy becomes positive within a few weeks of onset, before radiographs show mineralization, and is the best early confirmatory test. Serum alkaline phosphatase supports the diagnosis but is nonspecific, and radiographs lag the process. EMG does not identify ectopic bone.
  60. The correct answer is B (Polyphasic with six phases, because phases equal the number of baseline crossings plus one): A phase is a portion of the waveform between baseline departures, so the count is baseline crossings plus one, giving six here. A motor unit potential with five or more phases is polyphasic. Turns are direction changes that need not cross the baseline and are counted separately.
  61. The correct answer is C (Critical illness myopathy, with primary muscle fiber damage and reduced membrane excitability after steroids): Normal sensory responses with low motor amplitudes and small polyphasic early recruiting units indicate a myopathy, here after steroids and blockade in critical illness. Critical illness polyneuropathy would reduce sensory amplitudes, and Guillain-Barre syndrome would show demyelinating slowing.
  62. The correct answer is B (Serial casting with changes every few days to progressively lengthen shortened soft tissues): Little change under a nerve block shows the limitation is fixed soft tissue shortening, so toxin to reduce tone will not help. After failed stretching and positioning, serial casting is the best next nonoperative step, and surgery is held for casting failure.
  63. The correct answer is A (Methylphenidate, a stimulant that modestly improves attention and processing speed after traumatic brain injury): Methylphenidate has the most consistent evidence for improving attention and processing speed after traumatic brain injury, and he has no seizure or cardiovascular reason to avoid it. Haloperidol and tricyclic antidepressants block dopamine or acetylcholine and can slow recovery. Donepezil has weaker and less consistent evidence for attention than stimulants.
  64. The correct answer is B (Limit upright activity and use log rolling precautions until the surgical team decides on stabilization): Pain that is worse upright and a high instability score indicate mechanical instability, so axial loading should be limited with log rolling until surgeons decide on stabilization. Trunk exercises and lifting add stress. A flexible lumbar corset does not control motion at a thoracolumbar level.
  65. The correct answer is B (Mirels score, in which a total of nine or more generally favors prophylactic fixation): The Mirels score assigns points for site, pain, lesion type, and size of a long bone metastasis. A total of nine or more suggests prophylactic fixation, while lower totals favor observation or radiation. The other systems address survival, performance, or spinal stability.
  66. The correct answer is C (High risk, with continuous electrocardiographic monitoring and close staff supervision throughout early exercise sessions): Ejection fraction below forty percent, complex ventricular ectopy, and an exertional fall in systolic pressure are each high risk features. High risk patients need continuous electrocardiographic monitoring and close supervision. Home exercise or intermittent monitoring would miss dangerous arrhythmias.
  67. The correct answer is B (Motor responses disappear by about 10 days and sensory responses by about 11 days): After complete axonal transection the distal stump keeps conducting until Wallerian degeneration finishes, so the motor response is lost by roughly 7 to 10 days and the sensory response by about 10 to 11 days. Studies done in the first days after injury can look normal distal to the lesion. The very short timelines or the 3 to 6 week figures do not match the known sequence.
  68. The correct answer is C (Use a firm mattress, a thin pillow, and daily spinal extension and deep breathing exercises): Ankylosing spondylitis tends to fuse in flexion, so posture training uses extension and chest expansion exercises, a firm surface, and minimal pillow height. Flexion stretching, corsets, and prolonged bed rest all increase stiffness and favor a kyphotic posture.
  69. The correct answer is D (Firmer stool consistency through dietary fiber, with manual evacuation once or twice daily after meals): In an areflexic bowel there is no reflex defecation, so stool should be kept firm and removed manually on a frequent schedule to prevent overflow leakage. Softening stool further worsens incontinence between evacuations. Suppositories rely on a reflex arc that this patient lacks, and weekly evacuation is too infrequent.
  70. The correct answer is A (Tacrolimus, a calcineurin inhibitor causing neurotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, hypomagnesemia, hyperkalemia and diabetes): Tacrolimus and cyclosporine are calcineurin inhibitors whose dose-related toxicities include tremor and other neurotoxicity, nephrotoxicity, hypomagnesemia and hyperkalemia, so tremor with falling renal function and low magnesium points to tacrolimus. Mycophenolate and azathioprine mainly suppress the marrow, and sirolimus causes lipid abnormalities and poor wound healing.
  71. The correct answer is C (Custom pressure garment worn most of the day with silicone and a daily stretching program): A raised, itchy scar crossing a joint with early contracture is hypertrophic and needs early pressure garments, silicone, and stretching to limit scar bulk and preserve range. Steroid injection may be added later but should not replace compression and exercise. Waiting lets contracture progress, and surgery is reserved for failure of conservative care.
  72. The correct answer is D (Decrement caused by unstable immature endplates during reinnervation, so the diagnosis remains motor neuron disease): Decrement can occur in motor neuron disease and other neurogenic disorders because newly formed collateral sprouts have immature, unstable endplates. In this patient with widespread denervation and no ocular or fatigable weakness, the decrement does not establish a primary junction disorder. The hallmark of Lambert Eaton syndrome is increment after exercise, not decrement.
  73. The correct answer is D (L5 root, which innervates extensor hallucis longus and the dorsal foot skin): Great toe extension weakness with lateral leg and dorsal foot sensory loss and a preserved ankle reflex localizes to the L5 root. L4 compression would favor medial leg numbness and a reduced patellar reflex. A common peroneal neuropathy mimics the motor and sensory pattern, but the stem asks which root a herniated disc compresses.
  74. The correct answer is D (Terminate the test, begin a monitored recovery, and notify the supervising physician promptly): A fall in systolic pressure with increasing workload, together with symptoms such as lightheadedness, is an indication to end the test because it suggests inadequate cardiac output or ischemia. Recovery is monitored and the physician informed. Continuing, lowering the grade, or waiting at the same load prolongs the stress on a heart that is not responding.
  75. The correct answer is B (A direct oral anticoagulant started during this admission, preferred over warfarin for most patients here): In nonvalvular atrial fibrillation, direct oral anticoagulants reduce stroke and intracranial hemorrhage compared with warfarin and are preferred in most patients. Timing is individualized by infarct size, and this patient has no hemorrhagic transformation. Aspirin and dual antiplatelet therapy are far less effective at preventing cardioembolism.
  76. The correct answer is B (Discuss early gastrostomy placement now while respiratory function allows, with oral tasting as tolerated): In ALS, gastrostomy is best placed while forced vital capacity remains above about 50 percent, because respiratory decline raises procedural risk. She can keep tasting food for pleasure. Waiting until severe respiratory failure, using long-term nasogastric tubes, or stopping oral intake with parenteral nutrition each carry more risk or less benefit.
  77. The correct answer is D (Mirror therapy within graded motor imagery to retrain the cortical representation of the missing limb): Phantom limb pain involves cortical reorganization, and mirror therapy and graded motor imagery have evidence as nonpharmacologic adjuncts. Without a focal Tinel sign or tenderness, a symptomatic neuroma is unlikely and surgery is not indicated. Immobilizing the stump does not reduce phantom pain, and escalating opioids is not recommended.
  78. The correct answer is C (Sacroiliac joint overload from the extended fixation to the sacrum, increasing stress on the joint): Lumbosacral fusion increases motion stress on the sacroiliac joint, and pain after fusion usually presents near the posterior superior iliac spine and worsens with transfers. Solid fusion without stenosis argues against pseudarthrosis and adjacent segment stenosis. Cluneal nerve entrapment lies near the iliac crest and is less tied to loading.
  79. The correct answer is C (Long thoracic nerve from the C5 to C7 roots, supplying the serratus anterior muscle): Serratus anterior weakness from long thoracic nerve injury causes medial scapular winging that is most obvious on forward wall push or arm elevation, with no sensory loss because the nerve is purely motor. Trapezius weakness from spinal accessory injury produces lateral displacement and drooping, and rhomboid weakness produces mild medial winging with retraction weakness.
  80. The correct answer is B (Moro reflex, which is normally integrated by about six months of age): Extension and abduction of the arms with finger fanning after a sudden head drop is the Moro reflex, which normally fades by about four to six months, so persistence at nine months suggests an upper motor neuron problem. The asymmetric tonic neck reflex needs head rotation and produces a fencing posture. The parachute reflex normally emerges and persists, so its presence is not abnormal.
  81. The correct answer is D (Achilles paratenonitis, a peritendinous inflammation where the painful swelling does not move with the tendon): Paratenonitis involves the tissue surrounding the tendon, with crepitus, diffuse swelling and an arc sign that stays fixed during motion. In tendinosis the painful nodule moves with the tendon. Normal fibers on ultrasound argue against a tear.
  82. The correct answer is B (Targeted muscle reinnervation, moving residual nerves onto denervated muscle segments to create new control sites): Targeted muscle reinnervation redirects the amputated brachial plexus nerves into separate muscle segments, creating several independent signal sites that match the natural intent to move the elbow, wrist, and hand. This allows more intuitive, simultaneous control. Osseointegration helps suspension but does not add control signals, and neuroma burying addresses pain rather than signal availability.
  83. The correct answer is B (About 1.25 to 1.5 grams per kilogram daily, adjusted for renal function and wound burden): Pressure injury guidelines recommend about 1.25 to 1.5 grams of protein per kilogram daily for those who are malnourished or at risk, modified for kidney function. The maintenance allowance of 0.8 grams does not meet the increased demand of wound healing. Intakes of 2.5 to 3.0 grams per kilogram are unsupported and risk renal stress.
  84. The correct answer is C (Attritional extensor tendon rupture from friction against a roughened, dorsally prominent distal ulna): Sudden loss of active finger extension with a preserved passive range and no tenodesis effect indicates tendon rupture, here from attrition over the dorsally subluxed distal ulna (Vaughan-Jackson pattern). A posterior interosseous palsy spares radial wrist extension and lacks ulnar head pathology, and sagittal band rupture preserves tendon continuity.
  85. The correct answer is A (Insufficient lateral wall support in the socket, allowing the femur to abduct during stance): Lateral trunk bending over the prosthesis with lateral femoral drift and distal lateral pain points to an inadequate lateral socket wall, which lets the femur abduct and shifts the trunk. Equal length excludes a long prosthesis, and the other options describe sagittal plane problems.
  86. The correct answer is D (Position the arm supported on pillows and take shoulder range gently within pain free limits): In a flaccid hemiparetic arm the shoulder is vulnerable to subluxation and capsular injury, so the arm should be supported and ranged gently within pain free limits. Pulling on the arm and overhead pulleys traumatize the glenohumeral joint, and sustained adduction with internal rotation promotes a fixed contracture.
  87. The correct answer is D (Orthopedic referral for total hip arthroplasty because disabling pain persists despite adequate nonoperative treatment): Persistent disabling pain with advanced radiographic disease after a thorough nonoperative program is the standard indication for arthroplasty, and her comorbidities are controlled. Debridement does not help advanced osteoarthritis, repeated steroid injections risk joint damage, and opioids are not appropriate primary therapy.
  88. The correct answer is C (Serum thyroid-stimulating hormone to evaluate for hypothyroid myopathy as a reversible cause): Fatigue, constipation, weight gain, delayed relaxation of reflexes and a mildly raised creatine kinase suggest hypothyroid myopathy, so thyroid-stimulating hormone is the efficient first test and the cause is treatable. The normal sedimentation rate and systemic features lessen the likelihood of polymyositis, and biopsy, EMG or antibodies follow only if thyroid testing is unrevealing.
  89. The correct answer is A (Thoracolumbosacral orthosis of the underarm Boston type worn during most of the day): Curves with apex at T7 or lower in a growing child respond to an underarm TLSO such as a Boston brace. A Milwaukee brace is reserved for high thoracic apexes, a corset has no corrective effect on a structural curve, and a Jewett brace is aimed at flexion deformities.
  90. The correct answer is A (Catecholamines, cortisol, glucagon, and inflammatory cytokines, which raise metabolic rate and drive protein catabolism): Major burns trigger a sustained surge in catecholamines, cortisol, glucagon, and cytokines that raises resting energy expenditure and breaks down muscle protein. Anabolic hormones are used as therapy to counter this state rather than causing it. Fluid and calcium hormones do not account for the hypermetabolism.
  91. The correct answer is B (Compare median and ulnar sensory latencies from the ring finger at equal distances): Recording the median and ulnar sensory responses from the ring finger at equal distances places both nerves in the same hand under the same temperature. A latency difference favoring slowing of the median fibers is a sensitive sign of carpal tunnel syndrome. The contralateral comparison and motor latency comparison are less sensitive and do not share the same conditions.
  92. The correct answer is D (Lateral trunk supports with a contoured back and adjustable pelvic positioning, supporting trunk alignment): Seating for a flexible curve aims to support posture and preserve function through contoured trunk and pelvic supports. Bracing does not halt progression in Duchenne, fixed recline hinders arm use, and tight chest straps can restrict breathing.
  93. The correct answer is A (Grade 2, a deep ulcer reaching tendon or capsule without abscess or osteomyelitis): Wagner grade 2 describes a deep ulcer extending to tendon, capsule, or bone surface without abscess or osteomyelitis. Grade 1 is limited to skin, grade 3 adds abscess or bone infection, and grade 4 adds localized gangrene. The stem excludes each of those features.
  94. The correct answer is C (Begin active ankle motion and advance weight bearing in a walking boot per the surgeon): After stable fixation with a healed incision, early active motion and graded weight bearing in a boot per the surgeon prevent stiffness and muscle loss while protecting union. Impact drills belong late in rehabilitation after union. Prolonged cast immobilization and heavy early resistance are both harmful.
  95. The correct answer is A (Activation of large myelinated afferents that inhibit nociceptive transmission at the dorsal horn): Conventional TENS uses high frequency and low intensity to stimulate large A-beta fibers, which by gate control theory inhibit nociceptive transmission in the dorsal horn. Opioid-mediated relief is linked to low-frequency, higher-intensity acupuncture-like settings. Peripheral block and motor contraction are not the principal mechanisms.
  96. The correct answer is C (Incremental response after exercise or fast stimulation, with small cell lung carcinoma as the usual tumor): Proximal weakness, autonomic symptoms, reflex facilitation, and low resting compound action potentials describe Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome. A presynaptic calcium channel defect yields incremental amplitude after brief exercise or fast stimulation, and small cell lung carcinoma is the usual paraneoplastic cause. A decrement at low rates belongs to myasthenia gravis.
  97. The correct answer is A (Oral phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitor taken before activity, with counseling on blood pressure effects): Men with injuries above the sacral segments often retain reflex erections and respond well to oral phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors, which are first line when nitrates and interacting drugs are absent. Counseling about hypotension and, for high lesions, autonomic dysreflexia is needed. Injection and urethral therapy are second line, and testosterone does not address the neural cause.
  98. The correct answer is A (Ergonomic correction of the workstation with postural retraining and strengthening of scapular muscles): Recurrent trigger points despite treatment point to an uncorrected perpetuating factor, here a poor workstation and posture, so ergonomic change with postural and scapular strengthening is best. More injections treat the symptom only. Opioids are not appropriate first-line for chronic musculoskeletal pain, and botulinum toxin has limited evidence in this setting.
  99. The correct answer is C (Eye tracking with dwell selection on an on-screen keyboard, because ocular control is preserved): Eye movements are usually the last voluntary motor function lost in ALS, so gaze tracking with dwell selection remains usable as the hands fail. Hand-based direct selection and hand switches will stop working as weakness advances, and neck weakness commonly develops too, making head pointing unreliable.
  100. The correct answer is B (Neuropathic ulcer caused by repetitive mechanical pressure over an insensate plantar bony prominence): A painless punched-out ulcer under a metatarsal head with callus and loss of protective sensation is the classic neuropathic ulcer. Arterial ulcers are painful with cool, pale feet and absent pulses. Venous ulcers sit near the ankle with edema, and pressure injury over the heel does not fit this location.
  101. The correct answer is D (Glucose may rise for a few days afterward, so he should check his readings more often): Intra-articular corticosteroid is absorbed systemically and commonly raises glucose for several days in patients with diabetes, so closer monitoring is the sensible advice. Diabetes is not a contraindication, and the effect is usually transient rather than lasting weeks. Lowering the sulfonylurea dose would risk worse control during the rise.
  102. The correct answer is C (Ciprofloxacin, because fluoroquinolones can impair neuromuscular transmission at the junction and trigger myasthenic exacerbation or crisis): Fluoroquinolones carry a boxed warning for worsening myasthenia gravis because they interfere with neuromuscular transmission. Cephalexin and trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole are generally acceptable, although the latter requires blood count monitoring with azathioprine. Nitrofurantoin neuropathy is a different problem from junctional weakness.
  103. Plasma exchange is reserved for severe relapses that fail steroids. Oral low-dose prednisone is less effective, and immunoglobulin has no established role in acute relapse.
  104. The correct answer is A (Orthopedic referral for surgical debridement, since extended nonoperative care and injections have failed): After more than six months of comprehensive loading, shock wave and two injection types without benefit, surgical debridement is the reasonable next step. A repeat steroid injection risks tendon weakening and offers no durable gain. Immobilization reduces tendon capacity, and an unchanged loading trial has already been given.
  105. It suits a young ambulatory child with good strength, preserved selective motor control, and no fixed contracture. Tendon lengthening treats shortened muscle rather than the reflex input, and a pump is a drug delivery system, not a rootlet section.
  106. Observation with calf stretching is appropriate. Casting is reserved for fixed contracture, imaging for abnormal examination findings, and surgery only after prolonged failure of conservative care.
  107. The correct answer is C (Grade 2, with a more marked tone increase and easy passive movement of the elbow): A Modified Ashworth grade of 2 describes a more marked increase in tone through most of the range with the part still easily moved. Grade 3 requires considerable increase making passive movement difficult. Grades 1 and 1+ require a catch or minimal resistance limited to part of the range.
  108. The correct answer is A (Osteoporosis, because the lowest site T-score is at or below minus 2.5 standard deviations): The WHO densitometric definition uses the lowest T-score at the lumbar spine, total hip, or femoral neck, and a value of minus 2.5 or lower defines osteoporosis. Sites are not averaged. Severe or established osteoporosis additionally requires a fragility fracture, not a lower T-score threshold.
  109. Sleep testing should precede any added sedatives, which can deepen respiratory depression. Stimulants and more exercise would not correct the underlying cause.
  110. Osteomyelitis is excluded, so the patient can proceed to plastic surgery evaluation for flap coverage with nutrition and offloading maintained. Adjuncts such as hyperbaric oxygen or stimulation do not replace surgical closure, and unrestricted sitting would undermine any treatment.
  111. The correct answer is B (Estimate creatinine clearance with the Cockcroft-Gault equation and adjust the dose to that value): Low muscle mass keeps creatinine deceptively normal, so renal function is overestimated. Cockcroft-Gault uses age, weight, and sex to estimate clearance and is the basis for most drug dosing guidance. Normalized eGFR overestimates clearance in a small patient, and arbitrary halving ignores actual function.
  112. Andexanet alfa reverses factor Xa inhibitors such as apixaban and rivaroxaban, not dabigatran. Protamine reverses unfractionated heparin, and vitamin K treats warfarin, with neither acting on this drug.
  113. A definitive socket made on a bulbous limb will soon fit poorly. Delaying compression prolongs edema, and surgical revision is not indicated for normal postoperative swelling.
  114. The correct answer is B (Low molecular weight heparin once bleeding risk is acceptable, continued through early rehabilitation): Motor complete injury carries a very high thromboembolism risk, and low molecular weight heparin begun early once hemostasis is secure is the preferred prophylaxis. Low dose unfractionated heparin alone and aspirin are insufficient. Prophylactic filters are reserved for patients who cannot receive anticoagulation.
  115. The correct answer is C (Physical therapy with gentle stretching, soft tissue mobilization, and progressive shoulder range of motion exercises): A painful cord limiting abduction after axillary dissection is axillary web syndrome, which responds to stretching, soft tissue work, and progressive range of motion.   116. The correct answer is A (Acute disseminated encephalomyelitis, a monophasic postinfectious illness with encephalopathy and demyelination): Encephalopathy within weeks of an infection with large, poorly demarcated multifocal lesions that include the thalami is typical of acute disseminated encephalomyelitis in a young child. Multiple sclerosis lesions are usually smaller and ovoid, and encephalopathy is not typical at onset. The absence of optic, cord, or movement features argues against the other options.
  117. Callus based secondary healing occurs with relative stability such as casts, intramedullary nails, or bridge plating. Stress shielding causes bone loss rather than hypertrophic callus.
  118. A pick up walker needs repeated lifting that worsens fatigue, and crutches or a quad cane give inadequate support for ataxia.
  119. The correct answer is B (Elbow extensors, tested with the forearm held in a position of slight flexion): The ISNCSCI key muscles are elbow flexors C5, wrist extensors C6, elbow extensors C7, finger flexors C8 and finger abductors T1. The triceps therefore represents C7. Each neighboring muscle belongs to the adjacent myotome.

  122. Triceps is radial and flexor carpi radialis is median, both rich in C7. Sampling only median muscles cannot separate a root lesion from median neuropathy.
  123.   124. It responds to a repeat course of immunoglobulin. Chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy is considered only with three or more fluctuations or progression beyond 8 weeks.
  125. The correct answer is D (Normal amplitude persists, since distal axon degeneration is not complete until about seven to ten days): After a complete axonal lesion, the distal stump keeps conducting until Wallerian degeneration finishes, so the motor response below the lesion is normal for several days and falls by about seven to ten days. Studies on day four therefore cannot yet show the true severity. The sensory potential persists a little longer still.
  126. The correct answer is A (Increased water content with loss of proteoglycans and disruption of the type II collagen network): Early osteoarthritis shows cartilage swelling from increased water content, proteoglycan depletion, and breakdown of the type II collagen framework, which reduces compressive stiffness. Pannus-driven destruction belongs to rheumatoid arthritis, and type I collagen replacement is a later reparative change rather than the initial event.
  127. The correct answer is C (Warm the limb to the standard range and repeat, since cooling slows velocity and raises amplitude): Cool limbs conduct more slowly and tend to give larger sensory amplitudes, so a limb at 28 degrees Celsius can mimic mild slowing. The correct step is to warm the limb to the laboratory standard and repeat the study. Raising stimulus intensity does not correct a temperature effect, and the amplitude change from cooling is in the opposite direction to that assumed.
  128. The correct answer is A (Popliteal cyst from fluid distending the gastrocnemius-semimembranosus bursa in knee arthritis): A fluid-filled structure that communicates with the joint between the medial gastrocnemius and semimembranosus tendons is a popliteal cyst, commonly secondary to intra-articular disease, and the enhancement behind it reflects its fluid content. An aneurysm would show flow, a sarcoma would be solid or vascular, and a meniscal cyst lies at the joint line instead.
  129. The correct answer is A (Slower velocity and prolonged latency, with a larger amplitude and longer duration of the response): Cooling slows sodium channel kinetics and conduction, so latency lengthens and velocity falls, while the response becomes larger and longer in duration. This can mimic mild demyelination, which is why limbs are warmed to standard laboratory temperature. A smaller amplitude would suggest axon loss, not cold.
  130. Quetiapine and zolpidem do not target loss of REM atonia.

  133.   134.   135. The correct answer is B (Functional tremor, supported by distractibility and entrainment to the frequency of voluntary movement): Tremor that varies with attention and entrains to a rhythm tapped by the other hand are positive signs of functional tremor. Essential and parkinsonian tremors do not typically entrain, and enhanced physiologic tremor is fine and tied to a metabolic or drug trigger. Normal tone and reflexes fit a functional disorder.
  136. Manual removal alone suits areflexic lower motor neuron bowels. Oral laxatives alone give unpredictable results and soiling.

The olecranon and medial epicondyle are not the standard axis landmarks, and the radial head is too distal.
  138. Oral fluids are inadequate at this level of injury. Bicarbonate and diuretics are adjuncts at best and harmful if the patient is not first volume resuscitated.

Dilated cardiomyopathy is common and may dominate the picture even when limb weakness is mild. Surveillance therefore continues on a regular schedule regardless of walking ability.

  142. Widening the socket would worsen femoral shift, and a liner or pelvic band does not correct the socket shape that allows it. The shape change is therefore preferred.
  143. The correct answer is C (Single-fiber electromyography of a weak muscle such as orbicularis oculi, showing increased jitter with blocking): Single-fiber EMG of a clinically affected muscle is the most sensitive electrodiagnostic test for myasthenia, detecting increased jitter and blocking. Repetitive stimulation is less sensitive, particularly in ocular disease, and a 6 percent decrement is below the abnormal threshold. Conventional needle EMG and routine conduction studies are usually unremarkable.
  144. This shear is the key added mechanism over simple compression. Friction injures only superficial skin, and moisture is not the primary factor here.
  145.   146. Heterotopic ossification is possible after trauma but forms within muscle and does not produce a sharp conical bone end.   147. The Borg scale, combined with symptom monitoring, is the standard practical method, aiming for a moderate range of perceived effort.
  148. The correct answer is A (Joint angles and segment velocities are kinematic, while ground reaction forces and joint moments are kinetic): Kinematics describes motion without regard to cause, such as joint angles and segment velocities. Kinetics covers the forces producing motion, including ground reaction forces, joint moments and power. Electromyography records muscle activation, so grouping it with force data and calling moments kinematic is wrong.

Demyelination requires a much greater degree of slowing with prolonged latencies, dispersion or block. A junction disorder would not produce absent sural responses.

  152. A total of 8 or less is classified as severe injury. Scoring withdrawal as abnormal flexion would give 7, which keeps the same category but is the wrong total.

  154. This prediction helps decide between neurolysis and casting or surgery. Radiographs and EMG do not quantify reversibility.
  156. The adult Confusion Assessment Method needs verbal responses. A coma scale and a pain scale do not screen for delirium.
  157.   160. A needle stop alone does not exclude a pocket fill, and the side port must never receive refill drug because it delivers straight to the intrathecal space.
  163. The correct answer is A (Fear-avoidance beliefs driven by pain catastrophizing that lead to activity restriction and deconditioning): Believing that movement will cause harm, together with catastrophic thinking about pain, produces avoidance of activity and then deconditioning that sustains disability. This is the fear-avoidance model. The other constructs exist in chronic pain but do not match the belief about spinal damage described.
  164. The correct answer is D (Preserved sacral pinprick sensation predicts a substantially better chance of walking than light touch alone): This patient is AIS B because sensation is preserved without motor function. Preserved pinprick in the sacral segments, reflecting spinothalamic sparing, is associated with a clearly higher rate of motor conversion and walking than light touch alone. Absent anal contraction does not preclude recovery.
  165. The correct answer is B (Reduced plasma volume and blunted baroreflex response impair blood pressure maintenance when upright): Bed rest reduces plasma volume within days and blunts baroreflex responsiveness, so blood pressure falls when the patient is first upright. Venous compliance increases rather than venous tone, which adds pooling but does not explain the whole picture. Loss of the muscle pump contributes, yet it is not sufficient without the volume and reflex changes.
  166.   167.   168. The correct answer is D (Lower CMAP amplitude with proximal than distal stimulation, preserved distal amplitude, and no fibrillation potentials): Focal conduction block from compression at the spiral groove produces a drop in CMAP amplitude across the lesion, while distal amplitude stays normal and there is no denervation because axons remain intact.   169. The correct answer is D (Thymectomy, which lowers steroid requirement and improves clinical status even without a thymoma on chest imaging): A randomized trial of thymectomy in nonthymomatous, acetylcholine receptor antibody positive generalized myasthenia showed better clinical outcomes and lower steroid needs. Benefit does not depend on a visible thymoma. Plasma exchange is a rescue therapy, not a replacement for thymectomy.
  170.   171. The correct answer is C (Recommend gastrostomy placement soon, before forced vital capacity falls below fifty percent and raises procedural risk): Weight loss and declining swallow in bulbar ALS call for early gastrostomy, ideally before vital capacity drops to about 50 percent because procedural respiratory risk rises as it falls. Waiting until it is far lower makes the procedure hazardous. Nasogastric feeding is a temporary measure, and oral feeding alone will not reverse the weight loss.
  172. The correct answer is A (Voluntary shoulder abduction and finger extension detectable within the first 72 hours of stroke): Early shoulder abduction and finger extension strength predicts later dexterity, as used in the SAFE score for the upper limb.   173. The correct answer is D (Cystoscopy with biopsy, because long-term catheter use raises the risk of squamous cell bladder cancer): Chronic indwelling catheters and long duration of spinal cord injury increase the risk of bladder cancer, often squamous cell type, and painless gross hematuria needs direct evaluation.   177.   181.   188.   194. The correct answer is D (Loss of descending sympathetic cardiac input leaves vagal tone unopposed during airway stimulation): Cervical and high thoracic injury disconnects sympathetic outflow to the heart (T1 to T4) while the vagus remains intact, so stimuli such as tracheal suctioning provoke unopposed vagal bradycardia, a typical pattern in the first weeks. Hypoxemia can add to it, but the autonomic imbalance is the underlying reason. Normal troponin makes contusion unlikely.
  195.   200.   206. The correct answer is D (Spontaneous depolarization of a single denervated muscle fiber from membrane instability after nerve loss): Fibrillation potentials are action potentials of individual muscle fibers that lose their nerve supply and become unstable, firing spontaneously from the resting membrane. They are not motor unit discharges, so a whole unit firing from a distal axon describes a fasciculation instead. Channel defects that give waxing and waning discharges produce myotonia, not denervation activity.

  215.
