  242. The correct answer is C (Total contact casting with strict offloading until the skin temperature difference between the feet resolves): Acute Charcot neuroarthropathy presents with a warm, swollen foot and often normal early radiographs, and it is treated with immobilization and offloading, classically total contact casting, until the temperature difference falls to about 2 degrees Celsius or less. Footwear and gait training are introduced only after consolidation. Intact skin and palpable pulses make infection less likely than Charcot.
  243. The correct answer is B (A valgus-producing unloader knee brace that shifts load toward the lateral compartment during stance): A valgus-producing unloader brace transfers stance load to the less affected lateral compartment and can reduce medial compartment pain. A hinged postoperative brace controls motion, not load, and a patellar sleeve addresses a different compartment. Increasing medial compression would worsen her symptoms.
  244. The correct answer is B (Contrast MRI of the spine, to evaluate for vertebral osteomyelitis or epidural abscess): Fever, focal tenderness, elevated ESR, diabetes and a recent soft tissue infection raise concern for vertebral osteomyelitis or epidural abscess, and contrast MRI is the most sensitive test and shows epidural extension. Normal plain films do not exclude early infection. Bone scan, CT and electromyography are less useful at this stage.
  245. The correct answer is D (Grade 3 of 5, movement through full range against gravity without added resistance): Grade 3 means full range of motion against gravity but no tolerance for added resistance, which matches this patient. Grade 4 requires holding against moderate resistance, grade 2 requires full range only when gravity is eliminated, and grade 5 requires normal strength against full resistance.
  246. The correct answer is A (Apply the Budapest clinical criteria, because diagnosis rests on symptoms and signs in several categories): Complex regional pain syndrome is a clinical diagnosis made with the Budapest criteria, which require continuing disproportionate pain plus symptoms and signs in sensory, vasomotor, sudomotor or edema, and motor or trophic categories. Triple-phase bone scan, MRI, and sympathetic blocks can support the picture but are not required and are neither perfectly sensitive nor specific. Patchy osteopenia on radiographs is supportive but not diagnostic.
  247. The correct answer is C (Intermittent pneumatic compression sleeves applied to both legs while she remains nonambulatory and bedbound): In immobile stroke patients, intermittent pneumatic compression reduced deep vein thrombosis in a large randomized trial and is the best supported measure. Graduated compression stockings alone did not reduce thrombosis and increased skin complications. Aspirin has limited venous effect, and mobilization is not possible when she cannot walk.
  248. The correct answer is D (Low load prolonged stretch held for hours, as with sustained positioning, splinting, or casting): Contractures respond best to low load prolonged stretch, which allows viscoelastic creep and sarcomere addition, and this is the basis for splints and serial casts. Brief intermittent stretching, even forceful, does not change resting length meaningfully, and ballistic stretching can provoke reflex contraction.
  249. The correct answer is D (Obtain emergency lumbar and sacral MRI to exclude cauda equina compression despite positive functional signs): Urinary retention, saddle sensory loss, decreased rectal tone, and absent ankle reflexes are objective signs of cauda equina syndrome, which needs emergency imaging. Functional and structural disease can coexist, so positive functional signs do not rule it out. Delaying imaging for outpatient testing or psychiatric referral risks permanent deficit.
  250. The correct answer is A (Duloxetine, because it treats both the pain and the coexisting depressive symptoms): Duloxetine is approved for fibromyalgia and also treats major depression, so one agent covers two problems. Pregabalin is also approved and helps sleep, but it does not address the mood disorder. Opioids and corticosteroids are not recommended because fibromyalgia is a disorder of central pain processing rather than inflammation.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Shortwave diathermy to the lumbar region because electromagnetic fields can interfere with the pacemaker): Shortwave diathermy produces electromagnetic energy that can disrupt pacemaker function and heat leads and tissue, so it is contraindicated in implanted device carriers. Superficial heat and cold on intact skin are acceptable. Ultrasound is also avoided over the device, but a lumbar application far from it is generally allowed.
  2. The correct answer is D (A 24 foot ramp, using the one to twelve slope recommended for independent propulsion): The accessibility guideline of one inch of rise for every twelve inches of run gives 24 feet for a 24 inch rise, a slope most manual wheelchair users can climb alone. Steeper slopes demand assistance or are limited to very short rises. A one to twenty slope needs far more yard space than he has and is not required for wheelchair use.
  3. The correct answer is A (Glucocorticoid myopathy, with type II fiber atrophy and typically normal enzymes and needle EMG): Chronic glucocorticoid exposure causes painless proximal weakness with type II fiber atrophy, normal creatine kinase and a normal or nearly normal needle EMG, and management is dose reduction with exercise. Polymyositis would raise the creatine kinase and show irritable myopathic units. Escalating prednisone for a presumed flare would worsen the myopathy.
  4. The correct answer is D (Neurologic and ambulatory function at the start of treatment, since pretreatment motor status predicts recovery): The strongest predictor of walking after cord compression is neurologic and ambulatory status when treatment starts. Patients who can walk before treatment usually keep walking, while those paralyzed rarely recover. Age, dose, and level number have weaker effects.
  5. The correct answer is D (Supervised exercise therapy with cognitive behavioral approaches, plus duloxetine if medication is needed): For chronic nonspecific low back pain, guidelines favor exercise, multidisciplinary or cognitive behavioral approaches and, when medication is wanted, options such as duloxetine, with opioids reserved for carefully selected cases after other options fail. Epidural injections target radicular pain, and fusion is not indicated for age-appropriate degenerative changes.
  6. The correct answer is C (Switch scanning on a dynamic display device with picture symbols, accessed through the knee switch): A child with intact comprehension but no motor route for pointing should start with symbol based vocabulary on a device she can access reliably, here a knee switch with scanning. Spelling systems are developmentally premature at age four, and evidence shows early AAC supports rather than hinders speech.
  7. The correct answer is B (It supports median neuropathy at the wrist and is weighed with clinical and electrodiagnostic findings): A swollen, hypoechoic median nerve at the carpal tunnel inlet with lost fascicular pattern supports entrapment at the wrist, and ultrasound complements rather than replaces electrodiagnostic testing. Nerves are much less anisotropic than tendons, so tilt does not explain the swelling, and the inlet location argues against a forearm lesion.
  8. The correct answer is D (Impairment measures medical loss of function, while disability reflects resulting limits in the worker's roles): Impairment is a medically determined loss of structure or function, rated with the AMA Guides. Disability is the resulting limitation in work or social roles and depends on occupation, hand dominance and context, so the rating alone does not define it. Compensation systems apply their own rules to convert impairment to benefits.
  9. The correct answer is D (Paroxysmal sympathetic hyperactivity from loss of descending inhibition of brainstem and diencephalic centers): Paroxysmal sympathetic hyperactivity follows severe brain injury and produces episodic fever, tachycardia, diaphoresis, and posturing once infection and seizures are excluded. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome and serotonin syndrome require exposure to the causative drug class, which this patient lacked. A normal EEG during the events argues against nonconvulsive seizures.
  10. The correct answer is A (Epley canalith repositioning maneuver directed at the right posterior semicircular canal to clear displaced otoconia): Upbeating torsional nystagmus with latency and fatigue on the right Dix-Hallpike indicates right posterior canal benign paroxysmal positional vertigo, treated by the Epley maneuver. The roll maneuver is for horizontal canal variants, and gaze stabilization exercises address unilateral hypofunction. Suppressants are not effective for this mechanical cause.
  11. The correct answer is B (Start nocturnal noninvasive bilevel ventilation, which improves gas exchange, symptoms and survival in DMD): Symptomatic nocturnal hypoventilation with a reduced forced vital capacity in Duchenne dystrophy is treated with nocturnal noninvasive bilevel ventilation, which improves gas exchange, symptoms and survival, usually alongside assisted cough. Oxygen alone can worsen hypercapnia, high-load inspiratory training is not proven in this progressive disease, and tracheostomy is reserved for failure of noninvasive support.
  12. The correct answer is A (A weight-activated stance control mechanism that locks the knee under load and releases for swing): Her falls come from stance-phase instability, so a weight-activated stance control knee, which resists flexion under load and unlocks when unloaded, directly addresses the problem. Swing-phase hydraulics and extension aids affect swing, and a free knee gives no stance protection.
  13. The correct answer is A (Acetaminophen at a reduced daily maximum, avoiding nonsteroidal drugs because of bleeding and renal risk): Reduced-dose acetaminophen is the preferred analgesic in cirrhosis, while NSAIDs risk hepatorenal syndrome and bleeding from varices. Opioids need lower doses and longer intervals because clearance falls and sedation can precipitate encephalopathy. The full four grams exceeds the usual limit of about two grams in advanced liver disease.
  14. The correct answer is C (Infraspinatus tendon, which maintains active external rotation with the arm at the side): The external rotation lag sign tests the infraspinatus, often with supraspinatus involvement, because the infraspinatus holds the arm in external rotation at the side. Subscapularis failure produces a lift-off deficit. The hornblower sign is a teres minor finding tested in abduction.
  15. The correct answer is C (Vasoconstriction and reduced cellular metabolism that limit edema and hypoxic injury to adjacent tissue): Cooling causes vasoconstriction and lowers tissue metabolic demand, which reduces edema formation and the extension of hypoxic damage into uninjured cells. Vasodilation and increased collagen extensibility are effects of heating. Cold decreases spindle firing and transiently worsens proprioception, so it does not protect against reinjury through that route.
  16. The correct answer is A (Aggressive control of vascular risk factors including blood pressure, glucose, lipids, and antithrombotic therapy): Stepwise decline with executive slowing and small vessel disease indicates vascular cognitive impairment, where the main strategy is aggressive prevention of further infarcts and white matter injury. Cholinesterase inhibitors and memantine have modest symptomatic benefit at best and do not treat the vascular cause, and nothing here suggests hydrocephalus.
  17. The correct answer is A (Steroid myopathy from type II fiber atrophy, which spares enzyme levels and routine needle electromyography): Prolonged high-dose glucocorticoid therapy causes type II fiber atrophy and painless proximal weakness, typically with normal creatine kinase and a normal or nearly normal needle study. Active myositis would usually show raised enzymes, tenderness, and abnormal spontaneous activity. Raising the steroid dose in this patient would worsen the weakness.
  18. The correct answer is C (Patellar tendon, medial tibial flare and popliteal region, with relief over the crest and fibular head): A PTB socket concentrates load on pressure-tolerant areas: the patellar tendon, medial tibial flare and the popliteal region, with relief over the tibial crest, fibular head and distal tibia. Bony prominences and the common peroneal nerve at the fibular head tolerate pressure poorly. Fully end-bearing designs belong to Syme-level limbs.
  19. The correct answer is D (Compensatory hypertrophy from a right pars defect, so evaluate the right pars with CT): A unilateral sclerotic, hypertrophied pedicle and lamina typically reflects stress hypertrophy opposite a contralateral pars defect, so the right pars needs imaging. Osteoid osteoma can also produce sclerosis, but extension pain in an athlete favors the pars lesion. Degenerative facet disease is uncommon in a 17-year-old.
  20. The correct answer is C (Support ventilation, stop the infusion, and consider cerebrospinal fluid drainage in intensive care): Baclofen overdose causes coma, hypotonia, and respiratory depression, so airway and ventilatory support come first, the pump is stopped, and cerebrospinal fluid drainage can lower drug levels. Naloxone and flumazenil are not antidotes for baclofen, and continuing the infusion adds drug.
  21. The correct answer is A (Scheduled prone lying and bed knee extension positioning, plus a knee extension splint overnight): Sustained extension positioning, such as prone lying with a night splint, supplies the long periods at end range that prevent hamstring and capsular shortening. Pillows under the knee and elevating leg rests hold the joint flexed for many hours, which promotes the contracture.
  22. The correct answer is A (About 3.5 mL of oxygen per kilogram of body weight each minute): One MET equals roughly 3.5 mL of oxygen per kilogram per minute, the resting uptake of a seated adult, so workloads are expressed as multiples of it. Seven mL per kilogram per minute would be about two METs, and an hourly unit does not match how oxygen uptake is reported.
  23. The correct answer is C (Infant botulism, with low compound muscle action potentials that increment after fast stimulation or exercise): Constipation, weak suck and cry, descending hypotonia, sluggish pupils, and honey exposure indicate infant botulism, a presynaptic blockade of acetylcholine release. Studies show small compound action potentials with facilitation after fast stimulation or exercise. Spinal muscular atrophy and Guillain-Barre syndrome do not produce this autonomic pattern.
  24. The correct answer is C (Scapular stabilization and rotator cuff strengthening with activity modification and short-term analgesics): A positive Hawkins sign with preserved strength suggests subacromial impingement without a full cuff tear, and first line care is rehabilitation emphasizing scapular control and cuff strengthening. Repeated injections risk tendon damage. Prolonged sling use invites adhesive capsulitis.
  25. The correct answer is B (Begin early enteral feeding through a feeding tube within the first day, providing high protein): Early enteral feeding, started within roughly the first day after resuscitation, blunts the hypermetabolic response, maintains gut integrity, and supports wound healing. Burn patients need high protein, not restriction. Parenteral nutrition is reserved for those who cannot tolerate enteral feeding.
  26. The correct answer is B (Vestibular rehabilitation with gaze stabilization and habituation exercises to promote central compensation for the deficit): Persistent imbalance and oscillopsia after vestibular neuritis with an abnormal head impulse test reflect unilateral vestibular hypofunction, which improves with vestibular rehabilitation that encourages central compensation. Prolonged suppressants and activity restriction slow that process. A canalith maneuver treats positional vertigo from displaced otoconia, which is not the problem here.
  27. The correct answer is C (Ia afferents synapse monosynaptically on alpha motor neurons, discharging orthodromically to muscle): The H reflex is the electrical analogue of the ankle jerk: Ia afferents excite alpha motor neurons monosynaptically. Low intensity stimulation recruits large Ia fibers before motor axons. The F wave, in contrast, is generated by antidromic backfiring and has no afferent limb.
  28. The correct answer is B (Levetiracetam, a first-line agent for suppressing chronic posthypoxic action myoclonus without much sedation): Action-triggered jerks after a hypoxic-ischemic insult describe Lance-Adams syndrome, which is treated with levetiracetam, valproate, or clonazepam. Carbamazepine can worsen myoclonus. Baclofen treats spasticity, not cortical myoclonus, and dopamine blockers add risk without benefit.
  29. The correct answer is D (Low baseline CMAP amplitudes that increase with fast stimulation, with possible decrement at slow rates): The picture is infant botulism, a presynaptic block of acetylcholine release caused by toxin. Botulinum toxin gives small CMAPs that show an incremental response to fast stimulation or exercise, and decrement may appear at slow rates. Demyelinating neuropathy and chronic denervation produce different patterns, and a normal sensory study fits a junction disorder.
  30. The correct answer is B (New loss of independence in basic daily activities at discharge compared with function before the admission): Hospital-associated disability is a new decline in basic activities of daily living between pre-admission baseline and discharge, often despite resolution of the admitting illness. It is measured against the patient's own prior function. Delayed discharge and hospital-acquired complications are different outcomes.
  31. The correct answer is D (Reactive arthritis after a genitourinary infection, with bilateral conjunctivitis and keratoderma blennorrhagicum): Asymmetric lower limb oligoarthritis, dactylitis, conjunctivitis, and plantar keratoderma weeks after urethritis make reactive arthritis most likely. Disseminated gonococcal infection causes migratory tenosynovitis with pustules rather than this triad, and Behcet disease lacks the preceding genitourinary infection and keratoderma.
  32. The correct answer is D (Albumin falls with inflammation and fluid shifts, so it reflects illness severity more than intake): Albumin is a negative acute phase reactant with a half-life near three weeks, and it drops with inflammation, capillary leak, and hepatic or renal disease independent of intake. The two-day half-life belongs to prealbumin, which is also confounded by inflammation. Diagnosis rests on intake, weight loss, muscle loss, and physical findings.
  33. The correct answer is C (Weak hip abductors on the residual limb, which cannot stabilize the pelvis during single limb stance): Lateral trunk bending toward the prosthetic side compensates for inadequate hip abductor stability and moves the center of mass over the stance limb. Hip extensor weakness produces a forward trunk lean, and a flexion contracture or insufficient socket flexion produces lumbar lordosis rather than a lateral lean. Gluteus medius strengthening is the focus here.
  34. The correct answer is B (Duloxetine at a low starting dose, acceptable at this renal function and helpful for mood): Duloxetine is a first-line agent for painful diabetic neuropathy and also treats depression, and it is acceptable when the estimated filtration rate is above about 30. Amitriptyline is tempting for dual benefit but its anticholinergic and orthostatic effects are problematic with glaucoma and orthostasis.
  35. The correct answer is C (Start an angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor immediately and taper the corticosteroid, treating suspected scleroderma renal crisis): New severe hypertension with acute kidney injury and microangiopathic hemolysis in diffuse systemic sclerosis is scleroderma renal crisis, and high-dose steroid exposure is a recognized risk factor. Prompt ACE inhibitor therapy is the lifesaving treatment. Escalating steroids or delaying antihypertensive therapy would worsen the crisis.
  36. The correct answer is B (Ciprofloxacin inhibition of CYP1A2 metabolism markedly raised tizanidine exposure and its hypotensive effects): Tizanidine is cleared by CYP1A2, and ciprofloxacin is a strong inhibitor, so levels rise several fold and cause sedation, bradycardia, and hypotension. Normal temperature and lactate make sepsis less likely, and the timing points to the drug interaction. Fluvoxamine is another inhibitor to avoid.
  37. The correct answer is B (Ten or more beats below the detection rate and no higher than the tested peak): Training heart rate in a defibrillator patient is kept at least 10 beats per minute below the lowest detection rate, and not above the peak reached on a symptom-limited test, so exercise does not trigger inappropriate shocks. Rates near 160 approach the detection zone and exceed tested territory. Perceived exertion supplements rate targets rather than replacing them.
  38. The correct answer is D (Phenol motor point injection guided by stimulation of intramuscular nerve branches in the biceps brachii): A motor point injection places phenol on intramuscular motor branches, which spares most sensory fibers and lowers the risk of painful dysesthesia seen with mixed nerve trunk neurolysis. Injecting the radial nerve would weaken the wrong muscles, and lidocaine wears off within hours.
  39. The correct answer is D (Hydraulic or microprocessor ankle adapting resistance to slope, allowing controlled plantar flexion): Hydraulic and microprocessor ankles give damped, adaptive plantar flexion and dorsiflexion, which improves foot flat on slopes and stairs and reduces near falls. A stiffer keel would make the foot even less adaptive, and a softer heel helps only at heel strike. A torsion adapter reduces shear in rotation but does not change sagittal slope behavior.
  40. The correct answer is B (Take doses only in daytime and avoid taking one within four hours of bedtime): Midodrine is an alpha agonist whose main adverse effect is supine hypertension, so it is taken while upright and during the day, with the last dose several hours before bedtime and the head of the bed raised for sleep. Dosing at bedtime or lying flat after a dose increases the hypertension, and meal timing does not change this risk.
  41. The correct answer is D (Orthopedic hamstring lengthening with distal femoral procedures, since fixed contracture and weakness limit rhizotomy benefit): Rhizotomy works best in younger children with good strength and no fixed contracture, which this girl lacks. A fixed knee flexion contracture with a crouched gait calls for orthopedic correction of the shortened hamstrings, often with distal femoral extension osteotomy or growth modulation. Toxin and a pump lower tone but do not lengthen shortened tissue.
  42. The correct answer is D (Weber syndrome, from a midbrain lesion involving the oculomotor fascicles and the cerebral peduncle): Weber syndrome combines an ipsilateral oculomotor palsy with contralateral hemiparesis from damage to the cerebral peduncle, usually from paramedian branches of the posterior cerebral artery or basilar tip. Benedikt and Claude syndromes add tremor or ataxia from red nucleus involvement. Millard-Gubler is pontine, with sixth and seventh nerve palsies.
  43. The correct answer is B (Record about ten to twenty supramaximal stimulations and take the shortest latency among the F waves): Different anterior horn cells backfire with each stimulus, so latency and shape change from trace to trace. Reference values are based on the shortest latency from a series of roughly ten to twenty supramaximal stimuli. A single trace or a mean latency does not match how normative values were derived.
  44. The correct answer is B (Vaulting, a compensation that raises the body on the sound limb so the prosthetic foot clears): Vaulting is rising onto the toes of the sound foot so the body is lifted and the prosthesis can swing through with the foot clear of the floor. Circumduction swings the prosthesis in a lateral arc, and hip hiking lifts the pelvis on the prosthetic side. Fear of toe catching is a common cause, along with a long prosthesis, poor suspension or high knee friction.
  45. The correct answer is C (Power tilt in space with recline, used at scheduled intervals for sufficient reperfusion time): A complete C5 injury leaves no triceps or trunk control, so independent push-ups and forward leans are not feasible. A power tilt in space and recline system offloads the ischia independently and reliably at scheduled intervals. A single daily shift is far too infrequent to protect the skin.
  46. The correct answer is B (Staphylococcus aureus, which includes methicillin resistant strains in patients with prior healthcare contact): Staphylococcus aureus is the most frequent cause of native joint septic arthritis in adults, with resistant strains more common after healthcare exposure. Gonococcus, pneumococcus, and Pseudomonas are important in particular settings but are not the leading overall cause.
  47. The correct answer is D (Absent voluntary motor units with fibrillation potentials not yet developed in this distal forearm muscle): After a complete transection the muscle shows no voluntary units at once, but spontaneous activity takes about two to three weeks to appear, and later in distal muscles than in proximal ones. At five days the needle study is therefore silent apart from insertional activity. Large long polyphasic units appear only after months of reinnervation.
  48. The correct answer is C (Fasciculation potential arising from spontaneous discharge of a motor unit, often from the distal axon): A fasciculation is the spontaneous firing of a whole motor unit, so its waveform resembles a voluntary unit and appears irregularly at rest. Fibrillations come from single fibers and are small. Myokymic discharges are grouped bursts that repeat in a regular rhythm, which is not described here. In a patient with normal strength and reflexes this finding is often benign.
  49. The correct answer is A (Flexor digitorum superficialis and profundus, which flex the proximal and distal finger joints): Finger fisting with flexed interphalangeal joints is driven mainly by the flexor digitorum superficialis and profundus, the usual targets for clenched fist deformity. Wrist flexors matter when the wrist is flexed, which is not present here. The thumb is free, so thumb muscles are not the problem, and the lumbricals and interossei extend the interphalangeal joints.
  50. The correct answer is B (Glenohumeral intra-articular corticosteroid injection followed by a supervised stretching program for capsular range): Diabetes, night pain and loss of passive external rotation with a normal cuff point to adhesive capsulitis. Early painful stages respond to intra-articular steroid combined with stretching. Subacromial injection targets a different structure, and manipulation or release is considered only after prolonged failed conservative treatment.
  51. The correct answer is D (A dynamic wrist extension orthosis with outrigger finger extension assist, preserving grip and release function): A dynamic orthosis that holds the wrist in extension and assists finger extension lets him grasp and release during recovery. A static cock-up splint helps wrist position only, leaving the finger drop that limits release, while resting and spica splints solve different problems.
  52. The correct answer is C (Reassure the parents because milestones fit his corrected age, and continue developmental surveillance): Born 12 weeks early, he has a corrected age near 11 months, when pulling to stand, cruising, and specific first words are expected, and his tone and reflexes are normal. Correcting for prematurity is standard until about two years of age. Imaging or genetic testing is unnecessary without regression, abnormal tone, or other exam findings, and gait training before readiness adds no benefit.
  53. The correct answer is B (Stop all heparin products, start a nonheparin anticoagulant, and send heparin-PF4 antibody testing): A platelet fall of more than 30 to 50 percent between days 5 and 10 of heparin with a new thrombosis gives a high 4Ts probability for heparin-induced thrombocytopenia. All heparin, including flushes, is stopped and a nonheparin anticoagulant started while antibody testing is sent. Warfarin alone can cause venous limb gangrene in this setting, and platelet transfusion does not address thrombosis.
  54. The correct answer is A (Steroid myopathy with type 2 fiber atrophy, managed by tapering steroid and adding resistance exercise): Proximal weakness after months of high-dose glucocorticoid with normal creatine kinase and normal needle EMG indicates steroid myopathy, which reflects type 2 fiber atrophy. Management is to reduce the steroid dose when the colitis allows and to prescribe resistance and aerobic exercise. Increasing prednisone would worsen the weakness.
  55. The correct answer is D (Posterior leaf spring orthosis assisting swing clearance while permitting some ankle motion in stance): Isolated dorsiflexor weakness with good knee strength and minimal plantar flexor tone is well managed by a flexible posterior leaf spring orthosis, which lifts the toe in swing and still allows natural ankle rocker in stance. A solid orthosis would be tempting for safety but blocks ankle motion and is reserved for greater tone or instability. Knee and hip controlling orthoses are unnecessary here.
  56. The correct answer is A (Critical illness polyneuropathy, since sensory potentials are lost alongside motor amplitudes, indicating axonal nerve injury): Low sensory and motor amplitudes with preserved velocities and denervation after prolonged sepsis indicate a length-dependent axonal polyneuropathy. Critical illness myopathy would spare sensory potentials, and a demyelinating neuropathy would show slowing, block or dispersion. A junction disorder would not produce lost sensory amplitudes.
  57. The correct answer is B (Return of the delayed plantar response and bulbocavernosus reflex within the first few days): Spinal shock resolves in stages, and the first reflexes to return are cutaneous ones, namely the delayed plantar response and the bulbocavernosus reflex. Deep tendon reflexes come back later, and hyperreflexia and spasticity develop over weeks. The bulbocavernosus reflex also helps decide when the level and completeness of injury can be judged.
  58. The correct answer is C (Contoured seat cushion with pommel and posterior tilt in space, limiting forward shear): A contoured cushion with a pommel controls forward sliding while posterior tilt in space moves weight to the back and off the sacrum. A tightened belt is a restraint, elevating legs with tight hamstrings drags the pelvis forward, and a sling seat with recline increases shear.
  59. The correct answer is B (Amitriptyline, a tricyclic with strong antimuscarinic effects fitting the peripheral and central findings): Dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, dry flushed skin, tachycardia, and confusion form an antimuscarinic toxidrome, and amitriptyline is the strongly anticholinergic drug on her list. Famotidine and metoprolol can contribute to confusion but do not explain the peripheral findings. Statin memory effects are mild.
  60. The correct answer is B (Interrogate the pump after the scan to confirm it resumed its programmed infusion rate): Many programmable pumps are MR conditional, but the magnetic field can briefly stall the rotor, so the pump is checked afterward to verify it restarted at the programmed rate. Emptying the reservoir or raising the dose creates avoidable risk of withdrawal or overdose.
  61. The correct answer is A (Vaulting, rising onto the toes of the stance limb so the longer swinging leg clears): Vaulting means rising onto the toes of the stance limb to effectively lengthen it, which lets the longer swinging limb clear the floor. Here the short left leg is the stance limb, so the raised heel equalizes limb length. Hip hiking, circumduction and steppage are alternative strategies that act on the swinging limb.
  62. The correct answer is B (Three-phase bone scan, which detects early heterotopic ossification before plain films turn positive): Warmth, swelling, painful loss of motion and a rising alkaline phosphatase after spinal cord injury point to early heterotopic ossification. A three-phase bone scan turns positive weeks before radiographs, so it confirms the diagnosis early. Duplex is reasonable to exclude thrombosis but does not confirm the cause of the contracture.
  63. The correct answer is C (Therapeutic footwear with custom insoles to redistribute plantar pressure, plus daily skin inspection): The remaining foot carries extra load and is the next likely site of ulceration, so protective footwear with pressure-redistributing insoles, daily inspection, and professional foot care are the priorities. Barefoot walking, self-trimming, and warm soaks all raise the risk of unnoticed injury in a neuropathic foot.
  64. The correct answer is B (Obtain polysomnography to document REM sleep without atonia and make the bedroom safer): Dream enactment in the later night in Parkinson disease suggests REM sleep behavior disorder, which is confirmed by loss of normal REM atonia on polysomnography, and the sleeping environment should be secured at once. Frontal lobe seizures occur at any time and are typically stereotyped. Empiric CPAP or an insomnia drug does not address the problem.
  65. The correct answer is C (Prescribe a rigid brace worn most of the day, lowering the risk of surgery): A skeletally immature adolescent with a curve in the bracing range that has progressed should be braced, and benefit is greater with more hours of wear. Observation alone risks further progression during the remaining growth. Fusion is generally considered at larger curves, and exercise alone has not replaced bracing for a progressing curve.
  66. The correct answer is B (Nonorganic behavioral signs suggesting psychosocial influences on pain, which do not by themselves establish malingering): Pain with axial loading or simulated rotation and inconsistent straight leg raise are nonorganic signs, which suggest distress, fear or psychosocial contributors rather than a structural cause. They indicate a need for psychosocial assessment and do not prove intentional deception. A normal neurologic examination argues against radiculopathy, and these signs do not identify the facet joints.
  67. The correct answer is B (Fibrillations and fasciculations with large long motor units across several regions and normal sensory studies): Motor neuron disease produces active denervation with fasciculations plus chronic reinnervation with large long units in multiple regions, while sensory studies remain normal. Small short units suggest myopathy, decrement with jitter implies junction disease, and fibrillations in one myotome with paraspinal involvement indicate a single root lesion.
  68. The correct answer is D (Arcuate fasciculus and supramarginal white matter, disconnecting receptive from expressive language areas): Fluent speech with good comprehension and poor repetition defines conduction aphasia, caused by disruption of the arcuate fasciculus or supramarginal region. Broca area injury would produce effortful nonfluent speech, and Wernicke area injury would impair understanding. Isolation syndromes spare repetition.
  69. The correct answer is C (Melodic intonation therapy using rhythmic tapping and intoned phrases to engage right hemisphere speech networks): Melodic intonation therapy suits nonfluent aphasia with good comprehension and relatively preserved singing, using intoned phrases and left hand tapping to recruit right hemisphere networks. Visual action therapy is for global aphasia, semantic feature analysis for naming deficits, and receptive drills do not match his strengths.
  70. The correct answer is A (Total contact socket loading the patellar tendon and soft tissue while relieving sensitive bony areas): Modern transtibial sockets use total contact and load pressure-tolerant areas such as the patellar tendon, pretibial muscle, and popliteal soft tissue, while relieving the tibial crest, fibular head, and distal tibia. Loading bony prominences causes exactly the pain and redness she describes. Ischial containment is a transfemoral concept.
  71. The correct answer is C (Corticosteroid induced myopathy of the proximal leg muscles, a recognized complication of prolonged dexamethasone use): Symmetric proximal weakness with normal reflexes, sensation, and creatine kinase in a patient on prolonged steroids is typical of steroid myopathy. Polymyositis would usually raise creatine kinase. Radiculopathy would produce sensory changes, and tumor hemiparesis would be unilateral with upper motor neuron signs.
  72. The correct answer is A (Tilt in space, redistributing weight to the back while maintaining his hip angle): Tilt in space rotates the entire seating system, shifting weight onto the back without changing hip angle, so shear is limited.   156.   157. The correct answer is B (Static progressive wrist extension splint at tolerable tension, adjusted gradually across daily sessions): Tissue remodels in proportion to the time it spends at its end range under gentle tension, so a progressive static splint adjusted as range improves is the best fit. A short therapy-only session or a neutral resting splint provides little lengthening, and a flexion bias splint reinforces the deformity.
  158. The correct answer is A (Sertraline, a well tolerated antidepressant that improves mood and therapy participation after stroke): SSRIs such as sertraline are first line for post-stroke depression because they are generally well tolerated and avoid the anticholinergic and orthostatic burden of tricyclics. Benzodiazepines can impair motor recovery and cognition, and bupropion lowers seizure threshold in a patient with a structural brain lesion. Watching for bleeding when combined with aspirin is reasonable.
  159. Acquired demyelination is typically multifocal and uneven, with dispersion or block. Velocities this low are too slow to be explained by axon loss alone.

  170. The correct answer is B (Myopathic process, because weak fibers force early recruitment of many small units at low voltage): In myopathy each motor unit has fewer working fibers, so many units are recruited early and the pattern is dense but low in amplitude with small short potentials. Neurogenic loss gives a sparse pattern of large units, and central lesions give slow firing with poor recruitment. A peak of about 400 microvolts is well below normal.
  171. The correct answer is D (Lower gabapentin to a renally adjusted dose, because accumulation of the drug explains this neurotoxicity): Gabapentin is cleared almost entirely by the kidney, so a dose suited to normal function accumulates in stage 4 disease and causes sedation, confusion and myoclonus. The remedy is a dose matched to renal clearance. Pregabalin is also renally cleared, levetiracetam needs adjustment, and duloxetine is generally avoided when clearance is severely reduced.

A normal graft makes skin contracture less likely to explain a hard block. Septic arthritis would usually bring fever and severe pain, and neuropathy does not cause a mechanical motion limit.
  234. The correct answer is D (Take fast-acting carbohydrate and recheck glucose before starting, since rapid-acting insulin is still active): With active rapid-acting insulin and a glucose under about 90 milligrams per deciliter, aerobic exercise carries a high risk of hypoglycemia, so carbohydrate is taken first and glucose rechecked. The 70 threshold defines treatment of hypoglycemia, not safe starting values. Waiting for very high glucose is unnecessary, and the absence of ketones means exercise is not restricted for hyperglycemia.
  235. The correct answer is A (Spinal dural arteriovenous fistula causing venous hypertension and congestive myelopathy of the lower thoracic cord): Cord edema with dorsal serpiginous flow voids in a middle aged man with a slowly progressive, exertion sensitive myelopathy indicates a spinal dural arteriovenous fistula. Venous hypertension causes congestive cord injury, and the mixed upper and lower motor neuron signs reflect cord and root involvement. A tumor would show a mass, and transverse myelitis evolves over days, not months.
  236. The correct answer is D (Evaluate and treat the skin lesion and noxious triggers before changing antispasticity medication): A sudden rise in spasticity in a patient with spinal cord injury should prompt a search for noxious stimuli such as pressure injury, urinary infection, constipation, or an ingrown nail. The new ischial redness is a likely trigger that must be treated. Escalating medication first can mask a worsening problem and cause sedation.
  237. The correct answer is D (Train external cueing, such as floor markers or an auditory metronome, with attention to stride length): Freezing in Parkinson disease improves with external visual or auditory cues that bypass impaired automatic gait and with large-amplitude stepping. Floor lines at thresholds and a metronome are practical, trainable strategies. Quick small steps worsen the problem, and weights or foam balance work do not address the cueing deficit.
  238. The correct answer is A (Add sock plies to restore fit, and refer to the prosthetist if volume loss persists): Weight loss shrinks limb volume, so the socket becomes loose and pistoning occurs. Adding sock plies is the first, simple correction, and persistent loss or a need for many plies calls for socket modification or replacement by the prosthetist. Removing plies would worsen the fit, and a rigid dressing reduces edema, not volume loss.
  239. The correct answer is D (Communicating hydrocephalus from impaired CSF absorption at arachnoid granulations after blood products obstruct them): Blood in the subarachnoid space impairs CSF absorption at the arachnoid granulations, producing communicating hydrocephalus with enlargement of all ventricles. A dilated fourth ventricle argues against aqueductal obstruction. Ex vacuo enlargement reflects tissue loss and does not carry raised pressure, and overproduction of CSF is very uncommon.
  240. The correct answer is A (Autonomic dysreflexia triggered by uterine contractions, which are noxious input below the lesion): Pressure surges that coincide with contractions and settle between them, with sweating and flushing above a T4 lesion, point to autonomic dysreflexia rather than preeclampsia, which would persist and show proteinuria or thrombocytopenia. Epidural anesthesia is often used to block the afferent drive. Pheochromocytoma would not track with uterine activity.
  241. The correct answer is B (Stop the bisphosphonate, obtain full-length femoral imaging or MRI, and protect weight bearing pending orthopedic evaluation): Prolonged bisphosphonate therapy with thigh pain and lateral cortical thickening suggests an impending atypical femur fracture. The drug should be stopped, the whole femur and the contralateral side imaged, and loading protected until orthopedics decides about prophylactic fixation. Stable density says nothing about cortical stress injury, and a more potent agent would add risk.
  242. The correct answer is C (Instrumental swallow study such as fiberoptic endoscopy, since bedside screening cannot exclude silent aspiration): Bedside screening and dye testing cannot reliably exclude silent aspiration in tracheostomy patients, so videofluoroscopy or fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation is needed to guide diet. A wet voice after water already signals risk. An inflated cuff does not fully protect against aspiration, and thickening alone without objective testing is guesswork.
  243. The correct answer is C (Neutral wrist splinting at night with activity modification, since symptoms often resolve after delivery): Mild median neuropathy at the wrist in pregnancy is caused by fluid retention and usually improves after delivery, so night splinting is first line. There is no thenar atrophy or weakness to justify surgery, and systemic steroids are avoided when conservative measures can work.
  244. The correct answer is D (Diabetic lumbosacral radiculoplexus neuropathy involving several roots, the plexus and proximal nerves): Weight loss, severe asymmetric thigh pain and subsequent weakness with multilevel denervation including paraspinals and adductors indicate diabetic amyotrophy, a lumbosacral radiculoplexus neuropathy. A femoral neuropathy would spare adductors and paraspinals. Distal symmetric polyneuropathy begins in the feet, not the thigh.
  245. The correct answer is A (Periodic liver function testing because hepatocellular injury can progress to fulminant liver failure): Dantrolene is hepatotoxic and the risk is dose related and potentially fatal, so liver chemistries are checked before and during treatment. Renal clearance is the key concern for baclofen rather than dantrolene. Routine blood counts and ECGs are not part of its standard monitoring.
  246. The correct answer is C (Serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors, which modulate descending pain inhibitory pathways in the spinal cord): Serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors such as duloxetine, along with tricyclic antidepressants and gabapentinoids, are first-line for painful diabetic neuropathy, and the SNRI also helps her low mood. SSRIs lack consistent efficacy because they do not add noradrenergic action. NSAIDs do not target the neuropathic mechanism, and opioids are not first-line because of dependence and other risks.
  247. The correct answer is D (Tibialis posterior, an L5 muscle innervated by the tibial nerve, so abnormality implicates the root): Tibialis anterior, peroneus longus, and extensor hallucis longus all travel in the common peroneal nerve, so they are abnormal with either an L5 lesion or a fibular head neuropathy. Tibialis posterior shares the L5 root but travels in the tibial nerve, so denervation there places the lesion proximal to the fibular head.
  248. The correct answer is A (Anteroinferior labral detachment from the glenoid rim, known as a classic Bankart lesion): Anterior apprehension with a positive relocation test after an anterior dislocation in a young athlete indicates an anteroinferior labral tear, the Bankart lesion. A SLAP lesion causes pain with overhead throwing and not apprehension. Posterior lesions follow posterior instability events.
  249. The correct answer is D (Detection bias, because unblinded assessors may score outcomes more favorably for the intervention group): Outcome assessors who know each assignment may rate results favorably for the intervention, which is detection bias. Concealed randomization argues against selection bias, and the stem gives no information about dropout. Performance bias concerns differences in care, not the scoring of outcomes.
  250. The correct answer is C (Heterotopic ossification, which commonly causes warmth, swelling and reduced motion below the level of injury): Warmth, swelling, falling motion, a low-grade temperature and raised alkaline phosphatase in the early months after injury fit heterotopic ossification. A negative duplex argues against thrombosis, and a normal white count makes septic arthritis less likely. Charcot arthropathy appears later and causes joint destruction rather than isolated early loss of motion.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (A median-to-ulnar forearm crossover (Martin-Gruber) anastomosis carrying median motor fibers into the ulnar nerve): In a Martin-Gruber anastomosis, median motor fibers cross into the ulnar nerve in the forearm, so they are activated by median stimulation at the elbow but not at the wrist. This produces a response in an ulnar-innervated hand muscle only with proximal stimulation. A palmar anastomosis would not change the response between forearm sites.
  2. The correct answer is A (Nonparticulate dexamethasone, since its small soluble molecules do not aggregate and occlude end arteries): Particulate corticosteroids such as methylprednisolone, triamcinolone and the betamethasone suspension can form aggregates larger than red cells, and inadvertent intra-arterial injection into a radicular artery has caused spinal cord infarction. Nonparticulate dexamethasone does not aggregate, so it is preferred for transforaminal injection. Particle dilution or depot behavior does not make the other agents safe.
  3. The correct answer is B (Send fluid culture and start empiric antibiotics because crystals do not exclude coexisting septic arthritis): Crystals and infection can coexist, and fever with a very high synovial white count requires culture and empiric antibiotics until infection is excluded. Treating only the gout with anti-inflammatories or a steroid injection risks a missed septic joint, and allopurinol does not treat either process acutely.
  4. The correct answer is D (Noninvasive ventilation with bilevel positive airway pressure, started now to treat symptomatic nocturnal hypoventilation): Orthopnea, morning headache, and a falling vital capacity indicate hypoventilation, and noninvasive bilevel ventilation prolongs survival and improves quality of life in ALS. Oxygen alone can worsen carbon dioxide retention. A cough assist device helps when cough is weak, which is not her problem, and tracheostomy is a later option.
  5. The correct answer is D (Preganglionic root avulsion, since the sensory cell bodies in the ganglia remain connected distally): A dense sensory deficit with normal sensory nerve action potentials means the lesion is proximal to the dorsal root ganglion, so a preganglionic avulsion is likely. Horner syndrome and a winged scapula support root level injury. Postganglionic injury would have lost sensory responses well before three weeks.
  6. The correct answer is C (Elevated substance P concentrations, which amplify nociceptive signaling in the dorsal horn): Cerebrospinal fluid substance P is repeatedly found to be elevated in fibromyalgia, consistent with amplified nociceptive transmission. Serotonin and norepinephrine metabolites are reported low, which fits reduced descending inhibition and explains the benefit of serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors. Excitatory transmitters such as glutamate are increased rather than reduced.
  7. The correct answer is B (Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, which inhibits CYP2C9 metabolism of S-warfarin and displaces it from plasma protein binding): Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole is a classic strong interaction with warfarin through CYP2C9 inhibition and protein-binding displacement, and it can double the INR. Nitrofurantoin, cephalexin, and fosfomycin rarely cause large INR changes. If the combination is unavoidable, the warfarin dose and INR monitoring should be adjusted.
  8. The correct answer is B (Anisotropy, in which less sound returns to the transducer when the beam is not perpendicular): Tendons are anisotropic: they appear bright only when the beam strikes the fibers at ninety degrees, and tilting even slightly sends echoes away from the transducer so the tendon looks falsely dark. Rocking the probe back restores the fibrillar pattern. Shadowing, enhancement and reverberation are artifacts that do not change with probe angle.
  9. The correct answer is D (Bilateral obturator nerve phenol neurolysis, accepting lasting effect given her nonambulatory state and sensory loss): Phenol neurolysis is well suited to a nonambulatory patient with focal adductor spasticity, since weakness and sensory side effects matter little and the effect lasts months. Shortening the botulinum toxin interval and raising the dose increases the risk of neutralizing antibodies. Oral baclofen would add sedation to a drowsy resident.
  10. The correct answer is A (Use a moderate perceived exertion rating, checking heart rate against the beta blocked test result): Beta blockade lowers resting and peak heart rate and makes age-predicted maximum formulas unreliable, so the Borg rating of perceived exertion, usually 11 to 14 on the 6 to 20 scale, guides intensity. Heart rate from the medicated stress test is still a useful cross-check. Stopping the medication removes protection after infarction and is not a rehabilitation decision.
  11. The correct answer is B (High risk from skeletal immaturity, so follow closely and brace if the curve progresses): Risk of progression is greatest in a young, premenarchal child with Risser 0 who has not yet reached peak growth, even with a modest curve. Close radiographic follow-up with bracing if the curve progresses is the standard approach. Discharge ignores the growth remaining, and surgery is not indicated at this magnitude.
  12. The correct answer is B (Behavioral therapy for tics with family education, reserving medication for impairing or injurious tics): Tics that are not causing functional impairment or injury are managed with education and comprehensive behavioral intervention, which includes habit reversal. Medication is kept for tics that interfere with function or cause harm, and then alpha-2 agonists or aripiprazole are usually tried before older dopamine blockers. There is no evidence that early drug suppression alters the natural history.
  13. The correct answer is A (Physiotherapy that shifts attention toward automatic movement, supported by a positive explanation of the disorder): Specialized physiotherapy for functional movement disorders retrains automatic movement patterns and avoids focusing attention on the affected movement, after a clear and positive explanation of the diagnosis. Strength training addresses a deficit he does not have. Walking aids can entrench the disorder, and confrontation implies deception and harms the therapeutic relationship.
  14. The correct answer is A (Treat sleep, mood and the anticholinergic medication first, then reassess cognition after those contributors improve): Post-COVID cognitive complaints with a normal screening score and normal neurologic examination are commonly driven by poor sleep, depression and sedating anticholinergic drugs, so these are treated first and cognition is reassessed. Imaging adds little without focal findings, a stimulant would be premature, and formal testing now would be confounded by the same factors.
  15. The correct answer is D (Progressive loading exercise with activity modification, since steroid injection trades early relief for poorer recovery): Lateral epicondylalgia is a tendinopathy, and progressive loading with activity modification gives the best recovery over a year. Corticosteroid injection relieves pain early but is associated with worse outcomes later. Bracing is an adjunct only, and surgery is not considered after six weeks.
  16. The correct answer is B (Transcortical motor aphasia, with nonfluent output and intact repetition from medial frontal or watershed injury): Sparse nonfluent output with preserved repetition and comprehension after an anterior cerebral artery infarct is transcortical motor aphasia, often with contralateral leg weakness. Broca aphasia impairs repetition. Mixed transcortical aphasia would also impair comprehension, and anomic aphasia is fluent.
  17. The correct answer is A (Full thickness burn, destroying the dermis, which generally needs excision and grafting to close): A dry, white, leathery, insensate, nonblanching wound indicates destruction of the full dermal thickness. Nerve endings are destroyed, so pinprick is not felt, and closure needs excision with grafting. Deep partial thickness burns remain painful and blanch sluggishly, and tissue loss to muscle or bone would be a deeper injury.
  18. The correct answer is C (Upper motor neuron reflex bladder with detrusor-sphincter dyssynergia, causing high pressures and poor emptying): A suprasacral injury leaves the sacral reflex arc intact, producing reflex detrusor contractions with loss of coordination of the external sphincter, which raises pressures and impairs emptying. Sacral lesions give an areflexic bladder with low pressures. Cortical lesions preserve sphincter coordination.
  19. The correct answer is A (Vascular claudication from superficial femoral artery occlusive disease causing exertional leg ischemia): A fixed walking distance, relief within minutes of standing still, and absent pedal pulses point to vascular claudication from arterial occlusion. Neurogenic claudication usually needs sitting or flexion for relief and is better with cycling, while compartment syndrome and venous claudication have different timing and relief patterns.
  20. The correct answer is D (Biphasic current at 50 hertz with intensity high enough to produce a strong tetanic contraction): For strengthening, NMES uses frequencies of about 30 to 80 hertz at an intensity that produces a strong, comfortable tetanic contraction, since muscle force depends on motor unit recruitment. Sensory-level or very low frequency settings do not recruit enough fibers, and interferential current at a carrier frequency is aimed at analgesia.
  21. The correct answer is D (Anterior cruciate ligament, the primary static restraint to forward displacement of the lower leg): A noncontact landing injury with a pop, rapid hemarthrosis and increased anterior translation with a soft endpoint on the Lachman test is classic for an anterior cruciate ligament tear. The posterior cruciate ligament would give posterior sag and a positive posterior drawer. A meniscal tear alone does not create anterior laxity.
  22. The correct answer is A (Stage 3: full thickness skin loss extending into subcutaneous tissue without exposing deeper structures): Visible subcutaneous fat with granulation and rolled edges, but nothing deeper exposed, defines a stage 3 injury. Stage 2 never shows fat. Stage 4 requires exposed fascia, muscle, tendon, ligament, cartilage, or bone, and unstageable injuries have a base hidden by slough or eschar, which is absent here.
  23. The correct answer is D (Remove the catheter and begin nurse-assisted walking several times daily with orthostatic checks): Tethers such as urinary catheters and orders that keep patients in bed drive hospital-associated disability, and he is clinically stable. Removing the catheter and walking him with assistance several times daily protects function. Weekly therapy and bed alarms do not supply enough mobility, and facility transfer is premature.
  24. The correct answer is C (Gluteus medius tendon tear, a degenerative abductor tendon defect producing weakness beyond simple bursal inflammation): Abductor weakness with a Trendelenburg sign and poor response to bursal injection points to a gluteus medius tendon tear, which ultrasound or MRI can confirm. Bursitis alone does not produce true strength loss. Normal foot dorsiflexion and sensation make an L5 root lesion unlikely, and osteoarthritis pain is typically felt in the groin.
  25. The correct answer is B (Dynamic response carbon fiber foot that stores energy in stance and releases it at toe-off): A dynamic response carbon foot stores energy as the keel loads and returns it at toe-off, supporting variable cadence and brief jogging. A softer cushion heel changes heel strike loading but gives no propulsion. Hydraulic and multi-axis designs address descent control and terrain, not push-off.
  26. The correct answer is D (Methotrexate, given weekly with folic acid supplementation to reduce mucosal and hepatic toxicity): Methotrexate is the anchor conventional DMARD for rheumatoid arthritis and is paired with folic acid to limit adverse effects. Glucocorticoids are bridging therapy, hydroxychloroquine is weaker alone for erosive disease, and NSAIDs relieve symptoms without slowing joint destruction.
  27. The correct answer is D (Effortful swallow with repeated dry swallows and alternating liquid and solid boluses to clear retained material): Pooling in the valleculae and pyriform sinuses with aspiration after the swallow is a residue problem, treated with effortful swallow, repeated swallows, and alternating liquids and solids. Supraglottic swallow protects against aspiration during the swallow and a chin tuck does not clear residue. Radiation fibrosis does not make exercises futile.
  28. The correct answer is C (Avoid oral NSAIDs because of renal risk and try topical diclofenac to the knee): Oral NSAIDs reduce renal perfusion, especially with an ACE inhibitor and existing kidney disease, so they should be avoided. Topical diclofenac gives local benefit at the knee with low systemic exposure. A proton pump inhibitor protects only the gastrointestinal tract, and celecoxib still affects renal prostaglandins.
  29. The correct answer is B (Order attended polysomnography to confirm sleep-disordered breathing and guide pressure titration): Snoring, witnessed apneas, obesity, and sleepiness after stroke point to obstructive sleep apnea, which is common after stroke and worsens recovery and blood pressure control. Polysomnography confirms the diagnosis and allows pressure titration. Stimulants or hypnotics would mask or worsen the problem, and a latency test does not identify apnea.
  30. The correct answer is B (CRPS type II, because the syndrome follows a documented nerve injury and spreads beyond its territory): CRPS type II requires the syndrome to develop after a definable nerve injury, with symptoms that extend beyond the territory of that nerve. Type I has no identified nerve lesion. The not otherwise specified category is for partial presentations that do not fully meet the criteria.
  31. The correct answer is C (De Quervain tenosynovitis of the abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis tendons at the wrist): Maximal tenderness and swelling over the first dorsal compartment with a positive Finkelstein test is de Quervain tenosynovitis. Intersection syndrome produces crepitus more proximally, and Wartenberg syndrome would cause sensory symptoms. Basal thumb osteoarthritis centers on the carpometacarpal joint with a positive grind test.
  32. The correct answer is B (Lifelong graduated compression stockings with ankle pump exercises, regular walking, and periodic leg elevation): Long-term graduated compression, ankle pump exercise, walking, and leg elevation reduce venous hypertension and ulcer recurrence, and stockings must continue after healing. Loop diuretics do not treat venous edema well because the fluid arises from venous hypertension, and an ABI of 1.0 argues against an arterial platelet-driven process.
  33. The correct answer is C (Parallel to the nerve course, so the heated zone lies along the nerve length): A radiofrequency electrode heats tissue around the active tip rather than at its very end, so placing it parallel to the nerve maximizes lesion length along the nerve and lowers the chance of missing a segment. A perpendicular electrode makes a small lesion that crosses the nerve at one point. Alignment with the beam is an imaging choice and not a lesion strategy.
  34. The correct answer is C (Chronic lung allograft dysfunction presenting as bronchiolitis obliterans syndrome from progressive small airway fibrosis): A progressive, irreversible fall in forced expiratory volume with obstruction, air trapping on expiratory CT and no infection or infiltrate defines bronchiolitis obliterans syndrome, the most common phenotype of chronic lung allograft dysfunction, and rehabilitation is adapted to the new ceiling while the transplant team adjusts therapy. Acute rejection and cytomegalovirus infection usually show infiltrates, while an anastomotic stricture causes central rather than diffuse small airway obstruction.
  35. The correct answer is A (Reassure her, advise activity with analgesics, and defer imaging unless red flags appear): Acute nonspecific low back pain without red flags improves in most patients, and imaging does not improve outcomes while often finding incidental changes. Staying active with simple analgesics and reassurance is recommended. Bed rest delays recovery, and opioids are not first-line.
  36. The correct answer is D (Repeat the diagnostic block with a different-duration anesthetic to confirm concordant, substantial relief before ablation): A single sacroiliac block has a meaningful false positive rate, so protocols for ablation usually require comparative blocks with different-duration anesthetics and substantial concordant relief. Partial relief after one injection does not meet that standard. MRI does not prove a mechanical pain source, and steroid effect weeks later mixes diagnostic and therapeutic aims.
  37. The correct answer is B (External cues such as floor lines or a metronome beat to initiate and sustain stepping): Visual and auditory external cues bypass the impaired basal ganglia automatic stepping pathway and reduce freezing episodes. Dual task walking tends to provoke freezing, and rapid small steps worsen festination. A weighted walker slows the patient further and does not treat the underlying cueing problem.
  38. The correct answer is D (True neurogenic thoracic outlet syndrome from lower trunk axonal loss, with median sensory fibers spared): Neurogenic thoracic outlet syndrome involving the lower trunk classically gives a low median CMAP with thenar wasting, a low ulnar SNAP, and a normal median SNAP, since median sensory fibers arise from upper and middle trunk. Carpal tunnel syndrome affects median sensory responses early. Radiculopathy preserves sensory responses, and an elbow ulnar lesion would not reduce the median CMAP.
  39. The correct answer is C (Modified barium swallow using multiple consistencies, because it can reveal aspiration without a cough response): Videofluoroscopy with barium of varied consistencies follows the bolus through all phases and is the study that identifies silent aspiration when no cough occurs. Endoscopic evaluation views the pharynx and larynx but loses sight of the bolus during the swallow and cannot show the oral phase. Manometry records pressures and does not image the bolus.
  40. The correct answer is A (Proximal to the dorsal root ganglion, as in root avulsion, sparing the distal sensory axons): Sensory cell bodies lie in the dorsal root ganglion, so a lesion between the ganglion and the spinal cord leaves the peripheral axon connected to its cell body and the SNAP intact despite dense numbness. A postganglionic lesion or a ganglionopathy eventually abolishes the SNAP. Preserved SNAP with sensory loss therefore localizes to the root level.
  41. The correct answer is B (Propranolol plus gabapentin or bromocriptine, with avoidance of known noxious triggers when possible): Recurrent paroxysms of tachycardia, hyperthermia, tachypnea, diaphoresis, and posturing after severe brain injury, with a negative infectious and seizure evaluation, define paroxysmal sympathetic hyperactivity. Beta blockers, gabapentinoids, bromocriptine, and similar agents reduce episodes. Antibiotics and dantrolene target other causes, and dopamine blockers can worsen recovery.
  42. The correct answer is C (Right trigeminal afferent lesion, because only stimulation of the affected side fails to evoke either response): Loss of both responses on both sides after stimulation of one side only points to that side's trigeminal afferent limb. Left stimulation drives the right orbicularis oculi normally, so the right facial efferent limb is intact, which also fits normal facial strength. The tumor location and the sensory loss support a trigeminal lesion.
  43. The correct answer is B (Compression garment therapy with scar massage and stretching, planning surgical release if range plateaus): Immature burn scar responds to compression, massage and consistent stretching, with surgical release held in reserve if progress stalls. Operating on immature scar often leads to recurrence, and immobilization without exercise produces further stiffness.
  44. The correct answer is B (Replace volume with isotonic saline, and consider fludrocortisone for continued renal sodium wasting): Hypovolemia with high urine sodium points to cerebral salt wasting, which is treated by replacing volume and sodium, not by restricting water. Fluid restriction is correct for SIADH but would worsen volume depletion here. Hypertonic saline is reserved for severe symptoms, and rapid correction risks osmotic demyelination.
  45. The correct answer is D (The pelvis drops on the left, opposite the weak right hip abductor, during right stance): The gluteus medius of the stance limb holds the pelvis level in the frontal plane. When it is weak, the contralateral pelvis falls during stance, so right weakness produces a left pelvic drop. Foot slap and knee hyperextension belong to other muscle groups and do not describe a pelvic drop.
  46. The correct answer is D (Abdominal binder and leg compression stockings with gradual, staged progression to upright position): Nonpharmacologic measures such as an abdominal binder, compression stockings, and gradual staged elevation are the first step for orthostatic hypotension after high spinal cord injury, and they are often enough. Midodrine and fludrocortisone are added when these fail, and rapid tilting without support provokes severe hypotension.
  47. The correct answer is A (Refer urgently for orthopedic prophylactic fixation and protect the limb with partial weight bearing): A high score with functional pain and extensive cortical involvement predicts impending fracture, so prophylactic fixation before radiation is favored with protected weight bearing meanwhile. Radiation and bisphosphonates take time and do not restore mechanical strength. Resistive exercise would add dangerous load.
  48. The correct answer is C (Attended in-laboratory polysomnography, which records sleep stages, airflow, effort, and oxygen saturation together): Polysomnography measures respiratory events, effort, and sleep architecture and remains the reference standard, especially with significant comorbidity such as stroke. Oximetry misses events without desaturation and cannot classify them. Multiple sleep latency testing evaluates narcolepsy and hypersomnia, and the Epworth scale only screens for sleepiness.
  49. The correct answer is C (K3, an ambulator with variable cadence who can traverse most environmental barriers in the community): K3 describes an ambulator with the ability or potential for variable cadence who can traverse most environmental barriers, matching this patient. K2 is limited to low level barriers and fixed or limited cadence, which he exceeds. K4 requires high impact or exceptional demands such as running, which he does not perform.
  50. The correct answer is B (Reduced recruitment with increased recruitment frequency, typical of motor unit loss from axonal injury): Normally a second unit joins when the first fires at roughly five to ten hertz, so a unit at 30 hertz with no second unit means too few motor units are available, which is reduced recruitment with high recruitment frequency. This is the pattern after axonal loss. Myopathy shows early recruitment, and central lesions show slow firing.
  51. The correct answer is D (Transient conduction block in demyelinated axons that worsens when core body temperature rises): This is Uhthoff phenomenon, in which demyelinated axons with marginal safety factor fail to conduct when core temperature rises. Symptoms resolve with cooling and do not represent a new lesion or relapse. Steroids are therefore unnecessary, and the treatment is cooling and avoiding heat triggers.
  52. The correct answer is D (Place a gastrostomy tube, since prolonged enteral support is anticipated and nasal tubes risk sinusitis): When enteral feeding is expected to last beyond about four weeks, a gastrostomy is preferred over a nasal tube, which causes sinusitis, nasal erosion, and patient discomfort. Dysphagia alone does not mandate jejunal feeding, and gastrostomy does not carry higher aspiration risk than nasogastric feeding. Nasoduodenal tubes remain short-term solutions.
  53. The correct answer is B (Winging that worsens with lateral arm abduction, together with weak shoulder shrug against resistance): Trapezius palsy from spinal accessory injury, a known complication of posterior triangle biopsy, produces winging that is more prominent with lateral abduction and a weak shrug. Serratus anterior palsy produces winging that is more prominent with forward elevation or wall push-up. Rhomboid weakness and suprascapular neuropathy have different distributions.
  54. The correct answer is D (Assess socket fit and electrode skin contact, since volume change and sweat degrade the signal): Daytime limb volume shrinkage and perspiration reduce electrode skin contact, causing weak and erratic signals at one site. The first step is to check socket fit and clean or reposition the electrodes. Higher gain only amplifies noise, and surgery is unwarranted before basic contact problems are corrected.
  55. The correct answer is D (MOG antibody-associated disease, which commonly causes steroid-responsive, steroid-dependent optic neuritis with disc swelling): Bilateral optic neuritis with prominent disc swelling, long anterior nerve segments, perineural enhancement, a normal brain, and steroid dependence is the classic phenotype of MOG antibody-associated disease. Multiple sclerosis optic neuritis is usually unilateral with a normal disc and short lesions. Aquaporin-4 disease tends to involve the posterior nerve and chiasm with worse recovery, and chronic relapsing inflammatory optic neuropathy is considered only after antibody testing is negative.
  56. The correct answer is C (Common peroneal neuropathy at the fibular head with preserved ankle inversion and hip abduction): Weight loss and habitual leg crossing predispose to compression at the fibular head, causing dorsiflexion and eversion weakness with spared inversion from the tibial nerve. L5 radiculopathy would involve tibialis posterior and gluteus medius. A sciatic lesion would also weaken tibial muscles and reduce the Achilles reflex.
  57. The correct answer is A (A running-specific carbon fiber blade foot, which stores and returns more energy at higher speeds): Running requires a foot that stores and releases large amounts of energy at high speed, which carbon fiber running blades do far better than walking feet. Multiaxial feet and microprocessor ankles are built for terrain and walking, and solid ankle cushion heel feet suit low-activity users.
  58. The correct answer is C (Pad two finger widths below the axilla, grip at wrist crease, elbow flexed 30 degrees): The pad should rest two finger widths below the axilla so that body weight passes through the hands, with the grip at wrist crease level giving about 30 degrees of elbow flexion. Bearing weight in the axilla compresses the brachial plexus and causes crutch palsy.
  59. The correct answer is B (Polymyalgia rheumatica, because age, girdle pain, prolonged stiffness, and raised sedimentation rate fit): New onset girdle pain with stiffness over an hour, age above 50, and a markedly raised sedimentation rate point to polymyalgia rheumatica. Fibromyalgia has normal inflammatory markers and a longer, more diffuse course. Polymyositis would produce true proximal weakness and elevated muscle enzymes, and hypothyroid myopathy would not raise the sedimentation rate this much.
  60. The correct answer is C (S1 radiculopathy, since gastrocnemius and gluteus maximus are S1 muscles and the L5 muscles are spared): Fibrillations in medial gastrocnemius, gluteus maximus, and biceps femoris short head with a reduced Achilles reflex and normal L5 muscles like tibialis posterior and anterior localize to S1. A sciatic notch lesion would involve both divisions of the nerve, including the tibialis muscles.
  61. The correct answer is D (Botulinum toxin injections into the overactive neck muscles, repeated at roughly three month intervals): Botulinum toxin injected into the overactive muscles is first-line for cervical dystonia and relieves both the posture and the pain. Oral agents such as trihexyphenidyl help less and cause cognitive and anticholinergic effects. Deep brain stimulation and denervation are kept for patients who fail good toxin therapy.
  62. The correct answer is A (Grade II, neck complaint with musculoskeletal signs such as tenderness and no neurologic signs): In the Quebec Task Force classification, grade II is a neck complaint with musculoskeletal signs, which include decreased range of motion and point tenderness. Grade I has symptoms only, grade III adds neurologic signs, and grade IV is fracture or dislocation. Her paraspinal tenderness is a physical sign, which excludes grade I.

Primary hand osteoarthritis favors the distal and proximal interphalangeal and first carpometacarpal joints.
  64. The correct answer is B (Kinesiophysical, in which the examiner judges endpoints from observed biomechanical signs of maximum safe physical effort): In the kinesiophysical approach the evaluator watches for biomechanical changes such as altered posture and accessory muscle use to set the safe maximum. The psychophysical method relies on the patient's own report of perceived effort. Physiological methods use a heart rate limit, and isometric testing measures static force rather than determining dynamic lifting endpoints.
  65. The correct answer is A (Cervical MRI with prompt spine surgery referral, since the findings indicate degenerative cervical myelopathy): Hand clumsiness, gait imbalance, leg hyperreflexia and a Hoffmann sign indicate cervical cord involvement, so MRI and surgical referral are needed for degenerative cervical myelopathy. The EMG root finding is incidental to that picture. Traction or injection may worsen or delay care, and ALS evaluation comes only after cord compression is excluded.
