The correct answer is A (Screen for depression, posttraumatic stress, and sleep disorders and offer peer support alongside ongoing prosthetic care): Social avoidance, poor sleep and missed visits in a blast-injured veteran suggest depression or posttraumatic stress, so screening and peer support take priority. His device skill is already good, so more training hours will not address the problem. A myoelectric upgrade may help later but does not treat the underlying distress, and discharge would abandon him.
  67. The correct answer is C (Direct muscle stimulation, which shows reduced muscle membrane excitability in myopathy but normal excitability in neuropathy): Direct muscle stimulation bypasses the nerve: a reduced direct response means the muscle membrane is inexcitable, as in critical illness myopathy, while a preserved direct response with a low nerve-evoked response points to axonal neuropathy. It is useful when sensory studies cannot be recorded, as with severe edema. Repetitive stimulation and jitter do not separate the two reliably.
  68. The correct answer is B (Constraint-induced movement therapy, since he has enough active wrist and finger extension to practice intensively): Learned nonuse is a risk when the unaffected hand does most tasks. With adequate active wrist and finger extension and preserved cognition, constraint-induced movement therapy with intensive, task-specific practice promotes neuroplastic recovery. Early compensatory training and passive care forgo this window, and stimulation alone replaces the needed practice.
  69. The correct answer is D (Decline the evaluator role because treating and evaluating one patient creates a conflict of interest): A treating physician advocates for the patient, whereas an independent evaluator must be neutral, so one clinician should not hold both roles and she should be referred to another evaluator. Consent from the patient does not remove the conflict, and altering insurer review rights does nothing about physician bias. Detailed knowledge of her course is the very source of the problem.
  70. The correct answer is C (Hoover sign, where involuntary extension of the weak hip appears during contralateral hip flexion): In the Hoover sign, a limb that cannot extend voluntarily extends involuntarily when the opposite hip is flexed against resistance, which shows intact corticospinal pathways and supports functional weakness. The Babinski sign reflects an upper motor neuron lesion and Lhermitte sign reflects cervical cord irritation. Gowers sign points to proximal muscle weakness.
  71. Quadriceps and hip adductors indicate L3, and the absent tibialis anterior and medial hamstrings exclude L4. L2 would lack quadriceps function, and L5 requires knee flexion and hip abductor activity.
  72.   73. The correct answer is D (Approximately 60 meters per second, from the distance divided by the latency difference between sites): Segmental velocity equals the distance between stimulation sites divided by the difference in latencies, which removes the distal conduction and neuromuscular transmission time. Here 240 millimeters divided by 4.0 milliseconds gives 60 meters per second. Using a single latency includes time spent outside the segment.
  74. First-line care is load modification, hip abductor strengthening, stretching, gait retraining and a short course of anti-inflammatory medication. Surgery or repeated injections are reserved for refractory cases, and more downhill mileage would worsen the friction.
  75. The upper limbs are loaded more by wheelchair use and are relatively spared.

Equal halves do not occur at comfortable speed, and the opposite foot makes initial contact near 50 percent, not 40.
  77. Without fever, spreading erythema, or abnormal labs, systemic antibiotics are not indicated. Surface swabs cannot distinguish colonization from infection.

Early loading risks displacement and growth disturbance. Immobilization should not wait for MRI, which is rarely needed.
  79. The flexor carpi radialis reflex reflects C6 and C7. Hand muscles and most other upper limb muscles do not yield consistent adult H reflexes.

Calcium alone is insufficient. Estrogen is not an osteoporosis therapy in men, and restricting activity worsens bone and muscle loss.

  82. The correct answer is B (4.0, the risk in slow walkers divided by the risk in faster walkers): Risk in slow walkers is 20 of 100, or 0.20, and risk in faster walkers is 15 of 300, or 0.05, so the relative risk is 4.0. The odds ratio is about 4.75 and overstates the relative risk when the outcome is common. The risk difference is 0.15, and its reciprocal is the number needed to treat, not a ratio of risks.

Tumors, disc disease, and vascular lesions all need to be found promptly. Spinal fluid and genetic testing come later, and electrodiagnosis cannot image the cord.
  84. Splanchnic sympathetic outflow is unopposed because descending inhibition is lost, causing hypertension, while carotid baroreceptors drive vagal bradycardia and flushing above the lesion.

A flexion-biased corset would increase anterior compression. Scoliosis jackets and cervical devices do not control this mechanism.

  87. The correct answer is A (Refer to a driver rehabilitation specialist for vision, perception, and cognitive testing): Fitness to drive after stroke depends on vision, visual perception, attention, and judgment as much as motor strength, and left neglect is a major hazard. A driver rehabilitation specialist performs off-road testing first and recommends on-road assessment only if the findings justify it. Full motor recovery is not required, since adaptive equipment can offset weakness.
  88. Red flags signal serious pathology and are absent here. Blue and black flags describe workplace and system factors rather than the patient's own beliefs and mood.
  89. The correct answer is D (Reduced plasma volume with blunted baroreflex vasoconstriction, so venous return falls on standing): Bed rest contracts plasma volume and dulls baroreflex responses, so standing reduces venous return and causes tachycardia and presyncope. Plasma volume falls rather than rises, and the heart loses mass rather than gaining it.
  90. The hip is the more reliable site, and its T-score of minus 2.8 meets the densitometric definition of osteoporosis. Averaging sites is not part of the classification.

The best step is to stop the antimuscarinic, use behavioral bladder measures, and review the need for donepezil. A higher dose, a patch, or a further drug does not break the cascade.

  95. The correct answer is C (It heats dense collagen-rich tissue at depth that superficial heat cannot reach effectively): Therapeutic ultrasound is absorbed preferentially by tissues rich in collagen such as tendon, ligament and joint capsule, and it can heat several centimeters deep where a hot pack cannot. Slowed nerve conduction describes cooling, and eddy current heating of muscle describes shortwave diathermy. A hot pack warms mainly by conduction into the superficial tissues.

Opening pressure is typically normal in normal pressure hydrocephalus, so it does not confirm the diagnosis.
  97. Meningitis and carcinomatous meningitis produce cell changes or low glucose, and oligoclonal bands with normal protein point to multiple sclerosis rather than an ascending polyradiculoneuropathy.

  99. The correct answer is C (Roos extended arm stress test, positive when symptoms reproduce or limit sustained hand exercise): The Roos extended arm stress test places the shoulders in abduction and external rotation at 90 degrees with the elbows flexed while the patient opens and closes the hands for up to three minutes. It is positive when symptoms are reproduced or the patient must stop. Adson, Wright, and costoclavicular maneuvers rely on pulse change, which is unreliable and often positive in healthy people.
  100. Many of these products are marketed outside formal approval pathways. Honest counseling avoids promising cartilage regrowth and notes the cost and unproven status.
  101.   102. Pregabalin is effective for pain but can worsen peripheral edema and does not treat depression. Tramadol is not a preferred first agent.

  105. Transfemoral walking costs substantially more than transtibial walking, and both cost more than normal gait. Prosthetic knees help but do not erase this difference.

Fixed graded increments pushed through flares can cause relapse. Prolonged rest worsens deconditioning and offers no recovery benefit.
  109. Morning light and small evening melatonin doses move sleep earlier. Evening light would push the phase later, and sedatives or stimulants leave the timing problem untouched.
  110. A leftward turn therefore comes from the left splenius capitis working with the right sternocleidomastoid. Reversing the sides gives a rightward turn, and the other pairs produce tilt or extension.
  111. The correct answer is B (Safe transfers and mobility with caregiver training to return home at a moderate assistance level): Rehabilitation in advanced cancer emphasizes goals that match prognosis and patient wishes, such as safe transfers, caregiver training, and discharge home. Full restoration is unrealistic at this performance level. Limited prognosis does not rule out therapy that improves comfort and quality of life.
  112. Vitamin D supplements do not reduce falls in people with adequate levels. Hip protectors and alert devices reduce injury or response time but do not prevent falls.
  113. The correct answer is D (Slowed information processing speed with reduced sustained attention across demanding, time-limited cognitive tasks): Diffuse axonal injury disrupts distributed white matter connections, so slowed processing speed, reduced sustained attention, and fatigue are the typical persistent deficits. Focal syndromes such as naming difficulty or neglect imply a discrete cortical lesion. Isolated severe amnesia suggests bilateral medial temporal damage rather than shearing injury.

  115. Transient monocular vision loss is a retinal ischemic event from the ipsilateral carotid. Delaying surgery forfeits benefit, and anticoagulation offers no advantage over antiplatelet therapy here.

  117. The correct answer is A (Impaired selective motor control with reduced dexterity and fatigability of voluntary movement): Negative features are losses of function: weakness, loss of dexterity and selective motor control, and fatigability. Positive features are release phenomena such as spasticity, clonus, spasms, and cocontraction. Negative features often limit function more than spasticity does, so lowering tone will not restore them.
  118. Surgery is reserved for contractures that fail a full course of nonoperative treatment.
  119.   120. The correct answer is C (Low intensity aerobic activity such as walking or water exercise, increased slowly over weeks): Aerobic exercise started at low intensity and progressed slowly is the best supported nonpharmacologic treatment, and water-based programs are well tolerated. Beginning with interval or maximal resistance training commonly provokes flares and abandonment. Waiting for pain to fall before exercising delays the benefit, since graded activity itself reduces pain and improves function.
  121.   124. The correct answer is C (Withhold prophylactic antiseizure medication, since benefit for preventing post-stroke seizures is unproven): Guidelines do not recommend prophylactic antiseizure drugs after stroke because they have not been shown to improve outcomes and may cause sedation or impaired recovery. Treatment starts after an actual seizure, with late unprovoked seizures usually warranting long-term therapy. Cortical location raises risk but does not change this recommendation.
  125. The correct answer is B (Antidromic impulses reach the anterior horn cell and backfire a small fraction of motor neurons orthodromically): The F wave is a motor response produced when a few anterior horn cells are backfired by the antidromic volley and send an impulse back down the same axon. It involves no synapse and no sensory afferent, which is why it can be recorded with a supramaximal motor stimulus. The H reflex, by contrast, uses Ia afferents and a monosynaptic spinal synapse.
  126. Rewarming and repeating is the accepted remedy and is more reliable than mathematical correction. Diagnosing neuropathy from a cold study risks a false label.
  127. Peroneal muscles and extensor digitorum brevis cannot separate the two, and medial gastrocnemius mainly reflects S1.

  133. Heterotopic ossification removes calcium into tissue rather than raising serum levels, and malignancy or granulomatous disease lacks any supporting history.
  134. Equinus gives toe walking, recurvatum shows knee hyperextension, and stiff knee gait shows reduced swing flexion.

Alkaline phosphatase rises in many conditions, including fractures, so it is supportive but not specific. Computed tomography demonstrates established bone better than the early metabolic activity.

  139.   140.   141.   142. Propranolol, titrated carefully, has evidence for reducing aggression after brain injury. Phenytoin and further sedatives do not treat the behavior and may impair cognition.
  143. The correct answer is D (Borg perceived exertion near 12 to 14, since beta blockade blunts the heart rate response): Beta blockers and atrial fibrillation make heart rate an unreliable guide, so a rating of perceived exertion in the somewhat hard range is used. Formulas built on age-predicted maximum heart rate, including heart rate reserve, assume a normal chronotropic response. A fixed treadmill speed ignores day-to-day tolerance.
  144. The correct answer is B (Early return to normal activity with active neck exercises, education and reassurance, avoiding immobilization): Evidence supports early mobilization, active exercise, and reassurance for acute whiplash, because prolonged collar use and rest are associated with slower recovery and more persistent pain. Scheduled opioids are not recommended first-line therapy for this problem, and traction lacks good support in acute strain.
  145. The correct answer is A (Sensitivity of 50 microvolts per division, 10 millisecond sweep, low frequency filter near 10 Hz): Fibrillation potentials are small, so a high sensitivity such as 50 microvolts per division is needed, with a sweep near 10 ms per division and a low frequency filter near 10 Hz. A low gain would hide them, a long sweep would crowd the waveforms, and a high low frequency filter would distort them.
  146. The correct answer is C (Prescribe by perceived exertion in the moderate range because beta blockade blunts heart rate): Beta blockers blunt the heart rate response, so heart rate targets and age-predicted maximums underestimate his true effort. Perceived exertion in a moderate range tracks intensity reliably in this setting. Medication is not withheld to make a heart rate formula work, and normal tables ignore his own measured response.
  147. The correct answer is B (Repeated Galveston Orientation and Amnesia Test scores of 75 or higher on consecutive days): Resolution of posttraumatic amnesia is judged by serial orientation testing, most commonly the Galveston Orientation and Amnesia Test, with a score of 75 or higher sustained over consecutive days. A Glasgow Coma Scale of 15 can be present while the patient still cannot lay down new memories. The Rancho scale describes behavior and cognition but does not mark the end of amnesia.
  148. The correct answer is D (Shortened stance time on the painful left limb with rapid weight transfer to the right): An antalgic gait is a pain-avoidance pattern: the patient spends less time bearing weight on the painful limb and moves quickly onto the other leg. Stance time on the painful limb is therefore shortened, and the opposite step is hurried. Equal stance times would describe a symmetric gait rather than an antalgic one.
  149. The correct answer is B (Fibroblasts deposit type III collagen as new capillaries sprout and support the forming granulation tissue): The proliferative phase features fibroblast collagen deposition, angiogenesis, and granulation tissue formation. Debris clearance by neutrophils and macrophages marks inflammation, platelet plugging marks hemostasis, and collagen cross-linking with gain in strength marks remodeling over months.
  150.   151. The correct answer is D (A reproducible decrement in CMAP amplitude between the first and fourth or fifth responses): In myasthenia gravis, low rate stimulation around 3 Hz causes a progressive fall in CMAP amplitude over the first four or five stimuli because the safety factor of transmission is reduced. The bottom of the decrement is typically at the fourth or fifth response. Increment after exercise and low baseline amplitude characterize presynaptic disorders.
  152. The correct answer is C (Constraint-induced movement therapy with the right hand restrained during intensive shaped practice): Constraint-induced movement therapy suits patients with at least about 20 degrees of wrist and 10 degrees of finger extension, and it counters learned nonuse through restraint and shaped practice. This patient meets those motor criteria and has adequate cognition. Compensatory training and passive range of motion do not reverse nonuse of the weak arm.

  154. Compression at the suprascapular notch would affect both muscles. Quadrilateral space lesions involve the deltoid and teres minor, and upper trunk lesions affect additional muscles.
  155.   156.   157.   159. Very low continuous intensity gives little training effect. Lower-limb training is the core of pulmonary rehabilitation, and baseline FEV1 is not a prerequisite for starting.
  160. Treatment improves stability, for example by exchange nailing or added plating. Grafting addresses atrophic, biologically deficient nonunions.
  161. The correct answer is C (Slower than adult values, because myelination and axon diameter are still maturing at that age): Infant velocities are roughly half of adult values at birth and rise as myelination and axon caliber mature, reaching adult values around three to five years of age. Interpreting an infant study against adult norms would falsely suggest demyelination. Myelination is under way within the first months, not absent until late childhood.

Normal sensory responses fit a lesion proximal to the dorsal root ganglion. F wave changes are nonspecific, and thenar muscles are C8 and T1.

  174. The first step is to taper the most recently added and most psychogenic agent while preserving levodopa.   177. The correct answer is B (Take doses 30 to 60 minutes before meals and move most protein intake to evening): Large neutral amino acids from dietary protein compete with levodopa for intestinal absorption and for transport across the blood-brain barrier, delaying the response after protein-rich meals. Timing doses before meals and shifting protein to the evening is the simplest first step. Extended-release preparations have slower and less predictable onset, so they would not shorten the delay.
  179.   180. MRI of the whole cord is the diagnostic test. Carpal tunnel syndrome is common but does not explain rising symptoms and reduced reflexes.

  182. Cephalosporins, penicillins, and sulfonamides lack this interaction, and the proposed mechanisms for them are not clinically established.
  183.   184.   185. Binders and stockings then allow gradual progressive upright tolerance. Prolonged bed rest worsens deconditioning, and drugs or boluses are secondary.
  186. A levodopa trial is both diagnostic and therapeutic and should come before other agents. Baclofen and toxin treat tone or one muscle and will not fix the underlying deficit.
  187. Tricyclics carry anticholinergic and fall risks, gabapentin accumulates with low eGFR and sedates, and tramadol adds fall and seizure risk.
  188. Rimmed vacuoles with inclusions are typical, and the disease is largely resistant to immunosuppression. Polymyositis and dermatomyositis, in contrast, respond to steroids.
  189.   190.   191. Unresponsive wakefulness syndrome allows only reflexive behavior, and the plus and emergence categories require language-related or functional behavior.

  194. Erosive osteoarthritis has central erosions without skin or nail disease, and rheumatoid arthritis spares the distal joints and lacks proliferation.
  195. Activity driven by pain level keeps the pattern going. Prolonged rest worsens deconditioning, and pushing through flares with intense training tends to provoke them.
  196. The elbow and shoulder are less common here, although the elbow and shoulder feature more in brain injury. Recognizing the usual pattern helps in early detection.

Surgery is reserved for ischemia, perforation, or failure of decompression. Prolonged observation risks perforation, and stimulant laxatives add little.
  223. The correct answer is A (L3 and L4 medial branches, since each joint receives input from two adjacent dorsal rami): Each lumbar facet joint is supplied by the medial branch at the level of the joint's upper vertebra and by the medial branch of the level above, so the L4 to L5 joint is supplied by the L3 and L4 medial branches. L4 and L5 would instead address the L5 to S1 joint, and blocking one nerve leaves overlapping supply and an unreliable diagnostic result.
  224. The correct answer is D (Chemical or digital rectal stimulation to trigger reflex defecation through the intact sacral reflex arc): A lesion above the conus leaves the sacral reflex arc intact, so rectal stimulation with a suppository or digital technique can trigger reflex peristalsis and evacuation. Manual removal alone is the strategy for an areflexic bowel, where no reflex exists to recruit. Enemas are an adjunct for refractory cases rather than the routine trigger.
  225. The correct answer is C (Intravenous unfractionated heparin, since severe renal impairment makes enoxaparin prone to accumulate): With severe renal impairment, enoxaparin accumulates, so intravenous unfractionated heparin allows titration and rapid reversal. Standard full dose enoxaparin and rivaroxaban both risk accumulation, and warfarin without a parenteral bridge leaves the acute clot untreated while the INR rises.
  226. The correct answer is C (An intact lower motor neuron and peripheral nerve, which allows stimulation to elicit dorsiflexion): Functional stimulation for upper motor neuron foot drop works because the peripheral nerve and muscle are intact, so a pulse timed to swing phase elicits dorsiflexion. A fixed equinus contracture will not be overcome, denervated muscle responds poorly to standard pulses, and reduced sensation is not a reason to choose stimulation.
  227. The correct answer is B (Generalized pain in at least four of five body regions persisting for three months): The 2016 revised criteria require generalized pain in at least four of five regions, symptoms at a similar level for at least three months, and qualifying widespread pain and symptom severity scores. The tender point examination belongs to the 1990 criteria. A fibromyalgia diagnosis is valid regardless of other coexisting diagnoses, and no sleep study is needed.
  228. The correct answer is C (Obtain MRI of the tibia, which detects marrow edema and grades stress injury severity): MRI is the most sensitive and specific test for early bony stress injury and also grades the severity, which directs the length of relative rest. Radiographs are often normal for two to three weeks or longer. Bone scan is sensitive but less specific than MRI, so it is not the best confirmatory test here.
  229. The correct answer is D (Therapeutic ultrasound over the tendon, because acoustic energy does not disturb pacemaker sensing circuits): Pacemakers and other implanted electronic devices contraindicate shortwave and microwave diathermy, because the electromagnetic fields can inhibit sensing or reprogram the device even at low dose or with the coil placed away from the chest. Ultrasound delivers mechanical energy that does not interfere with the generator and can heat the tendon when applied away from the device. It is therefore the appropriate deep heating choice.
  230. The correct answer is B (It is lower from reduced fat-free mass, so standard equations tend to overestimate needs): After spinal cord injury, atrophy of paralyzed muscle lowers fat-free mass and resting energy expenditure, so equations built on able-bodied people overestimate needs. Spasticity does not offset the loss of muscle mass. Body composition, not weight alone, drives resting expenditure.
  231. The correct answer is D (Pursed-lip breathing with a prolonged exhalation, which keeps small airways open and lowers respiratory rate): Pursed-lip breathing creates back pressure that splints small airways open during expiration, which lengthens exhalation, reduces air trapping and slows the respiratory rate. Diaphragmatic breathing with weights can increase the work of breathing in hyperinflated patients with a flattened diaphragm. Breath holding and repeated forced huffs do not address expiratory airflow limitation in this patient.
  232. The correct answer is A (Pneumothorax from pleural puncture, so obtain an upright chest radiograph and monitor for progression): Pleuritic pain and dyspnea soon after needling the serratus anterior or thoracic paraspinals in a thin patient indicate pneumothorax from pleural puncture. An upright chest radiograph confirms it, and most small ones are observed. Soreness does not lower saturation, and an invasive chest tube is not the first step in a stable patient.
  233. The correct answer is A (Offer vertebral augmentation because pain is refractory to conservative care and the fracture remains unhealed): Pain that remains severe and disabling despite several weeks of conservative care, with marrow edema showing an unhealed fracture and no neurologic compromise, is a recognized indication to consider vertebral augmentation. Prolonged bed rest and opioids add risk. Open decompression and fusion are not warranted without canal compromise or instability.
  234. The correct answer is B (Ataxic dysarthria, with irregular articulatory breakdown and scanning speech from cerebellar pathway injury): Scanning speech with irregular articulation and abrupt loudness changes characterizes ataxic dysarthria from cerebellar injury. Spastic dysarthria is strained and slow, hypokinetic dysarthria is soft and monotone with rushes, and hyperkinetic dysarthria arises from involuntary movements.
  235. The correct answer is C (Start dexamethasone and obtain urgent MRI of the entire spine to plan radiation or surgery): Rapid weakness, a sensory level, and retention in a patient with metastatic cancer indicate epidural spinal cord compression. Steroids and urgent whole spine MRI are needed to guide emergency radiation or decompression. Delay risks permanent paralysis.
  236. The correct answer is D (Start an SSRI such as sertraline and add cognitive behavioral therapy during the rehabilitation stay): Persistent low mood with anhedonia, sleep and appetite change for weeks meets criteria for a major depressive episode, and combined SSRI plus cognitive behavioral therapy is first-line, with monitoring for suicidality in an adolescent. Amitriptyline adds anticholinergic burden to a neurogenic bladder and bowel and has more toxicity. Waiting until discharge delays treatment of a condition that worsens participation and outcomes.
  237. The correct answer is B (Serum calcium with intact parathyroid hormone to evaluate for primary hyperparathyroidism and hypercalcemia): Pseudogout in a young patient should prompt a search for metabolic disease, and kidney stones with constipation and fatigue point to primary hyperparathyroidism. Hemochromatosis, hypomagnesemia, and hypothyroidism are also associated with calcium pyrophosphate disease but do not explain the stones.
  238. The correct answer is D (The active electrode lies off the motor point, so depolarization arrives from a distance): An initial positive deflection occurs when the active electrode is not over the motor point, because the approaching depolarization is seen from a distance before it passes beneath the electrode. Repositioning the active electrode over the belly of the muscle restores a clean negative onset. Submaximal stimulation lowers amplitude but does not create a positive onset.
  239. The correct answer is C (Normal end-plate noise from miniature end-plate potentials near the junction, not evidence of denervation): Pain on needle advancement with a hissing sound and negative-onset potentials marks the end-plate zone, where miniature end-plate potentials produce normal noise. Fibrillations are regular, brief, and usually initially positive or biphasic at a quiet site away from the junction. Repositioning the needle away from the end-plate region clarifies whether true spontaneous activity exists.
  240. The correct answer is B (Supply 1.25 to 1.5 grams protein per kilogram daily using supplements and dietitian follow-up): Guidelines for pressure injuries recommend roughly 1.25 to 1.5 grams of protein per kilogram daily with adequate calories, delivered through supplements and dietitian support when intake is poor. Albumin reflects inflammation more than intake, and infusion does not improve healing. Excess protein with fluid restriction adds risk without benefit.
  241. The correct answer is C (10 percent, new cases divided by participants free of spasticity at enrollment): Incidence counts only new cases among those at risk, so the 120 prevalent cases are removed from the denominator: 88 divided by 880 is 10 percent. Dividing by the whole cohort understates it, 120 divided by 1,000 is the baseline prevalence, and 208 divided by 1,000 is the prevalence at the end of follow-up.
  242. The correct answer is B (The small L4 to L5 herniation abutting the traversing L5 root, matching the L5 distribution): A paracentral herniation affects the traversing root, which is numbered one level below the disc, so L4 to L5 affects L5 and L5 to S1 affects S1. Dorsiflexion weakness, dorsal foot numbness, and a normal ankle reflex fit L5, not S1. Herniation size does not decide clinical relevance, and the exiting L5 root is affected only by foraminal disease.
  243. The correct answer is C (Weight bearing as tolerated with early gait training, since the implant permits immediate loading): Cemented hemiarthroplasty gives immediate stability, so weight bearing as tolerated with early mobilization is standard and lowers complications. Restrictions based on bone healing apply to fixation of fractures, not to replacement. Precise partial loading is also unrealistic for a patient with cognitive impairment.
  244. The correct answer is D (A picture and symbol based display organized in topic pages tied to her daily needs): Her failure comes from impaired written language, not access, so the interface must shift from spelling to recognition of pictures and symbols grouped by topic. Eye gaze, key size and word prediction all leave the spelling demand in place.
  245. The correct answer is A (Cam morphology, a bony prominence at the head-neck junction that abuts the rim in flexion): An aspheric head with loss of the normal waist at the head-neck junction is the cam deformity, which jams against the acetabular rim in flexion and internal rotation. Pincer morphology starts on the acetabular side and shows a crossover sign or a deep socket rather than a femoral bump. Dysplasia is undercoverage and produces instability, not this contour.
  246. The correct answer is C (Increase calories and protein through dietitian assessment of intake and weight, not serum albumin trends): Serum albumin is an acute phase reactant that reflects inflammation more than intake, so it should not drive decisions. Wound healing needs individualized increases in energy and protein guided by dietitian assessment of weight, intake, and comorbidities. Intravenous albumin does not improve healing, and protein restriction is wrong with normal kidney function.
  247. The correct answer is B (Gluteus maximus weakness, which normally decelerates trunk flexion and extends the hip at heel strike): A backward trunk lurch at heel strike substitutes for a weak gluteus maximus by moving the center of mass behind the hip joint, avoiding a flexion moment. Gluteus medius weakness would instead produce a pelvic drop or lateral trunk lean. Normal knee flexion strength makes hamstring weakness a less likely explanation.
  248. The correct answer is C (Eccentric wrist extensor strengthening with a counterforce brace and modification of his backhand technique): Lateral epicondylopathy is a degenerative tendinopathy, and structured eccentric loading with a counterforce brace and technique correction is first line. Corticosteroid injections give short-term relief but worse intermediate outcomes. Surgery is reserved for failure of many months of rehabilitation.
  249. The correct answer is B (Urgent neurosurgical evaluation with head imaging, since findings suggest proximal shunt obstruction and raised pressure): Morning headache, vomiting and drowsiness with a slowly refilling reservoir point to proximal catheter obstruction and rising intracranial pressure, which is an emergency. Imaging and neurosurgical assessment are needed promptly. Infection usually brings fever or abdominal findings, and pumping a possibly obstructed shunt can worsen the situation.
  250. The correct answer is C (Zoledronic acid, a potent bisphosphonate, to suppress osteoclastic bone turnover and normalize alkaline phosphatase levels): Paget disease of bone involves excessive osteoclastic resorption followed by disorganized formation, with bowing, hearing loss, and a high alkaline phosphatase. Potent bisphosphonates, especially a single infusion of zoledronic acid, suppress turnover and are first-line for symptomatic disease. Vitamin D with calcium addresses osteomalacia, not this disorder.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (It attenuates low frequency components, reducing amplitude and shortening the apparent peak latency of the response): Raising the low frequency filter removes the slower parts of the waveform, so amplitude falls and the peak appears earlier. Lowering the high frequency filter would instead round the waveform and prolong the apparent latency. Excessive filtering therefore distorts the values being measured.
  2. The correct answer is A (Slower conduction velocity, prolonged distal latency, and increased amplitude of the sensory and motor responses): Cooling slows sodium channel kinetics, so velocity falls, latencies lengthen, and response duration increases. Amplitude typically rises because slower sodium channel inactivation temporarily increases the peak. Warming a cold limb is the remedy.
  3. The correct answer is B (Thyrotoxic periodic paralysis, in which excess thyroid hormone drives potassium into cells during attacks): Tremor and weight loss with hypokalemic attacks in a young man of Asian descent point to thyrotoxic periodic paralysis, confirmed by suppressed thyroid stimulating hormone and high free thyroxine, and treated by controlling thyrotoxicosis, nonselective beta blockade and cautious potassium. Familial hypokalemic paralysis shares the attacks but lacks thyrotoxic features. Hyperkalemic disease raises potassium during attacks.
  4. The correct answer is B (Set up a first-floor sleeping area with a bedside commode until stair climbing is safely trained): She needs minimal assistance and has not yet shown safe stair negotiation, so the bedroom and toileting should move to the entry level until stairs are trained and cleared. Guarding on stairs nightly places both spouses at fall risk, and a second railing does not substitute for demonstrated skill. Removing the cane would increase fall risk without any therapeutic gain.
  5. The correct answer is C (Arteriolar dilation raises capillary hydrostatic pressure, producing dose-related edema unresponsive to diuretics): Dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers such as amlodipine cause dose-related ankle edema through arteriolar dilation that raises capillary hydrostatic pressure, and the edema responds poorly to diuretics. Normal albumin and echocardiogram argue against low oncotic pressure and cardiac failure, and the other agents are not typical causes of this pattern.
  6. The correct answer is C (Hug the binders against the chest using both forearms so load stays off the fingers): Joint protection favors using larger, stronger joints and spreading load, so carrying binders on the forearms against the chest spares the inflamed metacarpophalangeal joints. Tight gripping or pinch loading stresses the capsule and promotes ulnar drift, and ulnar deviation under load worsens the typical deformity.
  7. The correct answer is A (Posterior leaf spring ankle foot orthosis assisting dorsiflexion in swing, with trim lines behind the malleoli): An isolated foot drop with preserved plantarflexion and stability is treated with a flexible posterior leaf spring AFO that lifts the toes in swing and permits some ankle motion in stance. Solid, double upright and ground reaction designs control more than is needed and add weight.
  8. The correct answer is D (Continue strengthening and defer cutting sports until strength and hop symmetry reach about 90 percent): Return to cutting sports is based on objective criteria such as limb symmetry of about 90 percent in strength and hop testing, plus psychological readiness, and her quadriceps deficit fails that standard. Young athletes who return before meeting criteria have a markedly higher reinjury rate. Time since surgery and bracing do not replace restored strength.
  9. The correct answer is D (Syndesmotic ankle sprain, an injury of the distal tibiofibular ligaments from forced dorsiflexion and rotation): A planted, externally rotated foot with pain above the joint, a positive squeeze test and positive external rotation stress is the typical pattern of a high ankle sprain from distal tibiofibular ligament injury. Lateral sprains follow inversion and tender the anterior talofibular ligament. Normal radiographs do not exclude a syndesmotic injury, so stress views or MRI may be needed if unstable.
  10. The correct answer is A (Nonoperative care with analgesics, activity modification and physical therapy, with close monitoring for progressive deficits): Most cervical radiculopathy from disc herniation improves with time, and mild weakness without myelopathy or severe progression calls for nonoperative care. Surgery is for progressive or severe deficits, myelopathy or failure of conservative care. Prolonged collar use is not recommended, and repeated testing adds no benefit.
  11. The correct answer is B (Flexible laryngoscopy to assess vocal fold mobility for a possible recurrent laryngeal nerve injury): A breathy voice and thin liquid aspiration soon after anterior cervical surgery suggest unilateral vocal fold paralysis from recurrent laryngeal nerve injury. Direct visualization of the vocal folds confirms it and guides treatment such as injection medialization. Swallow testing is useful afterward, but the cause is identified first.
  12. The correct answer is A (Place a retrievable vena cava filter, then anticoagulate and retrieve it once bleeding risk allows): A proximal DVT with a contraindication to anticoagulation is a recognized indication for a retrievable vena cava filter, followed by anticoagulation and filter removal once risk allows. Half dose heparin remains unsafe soon after hemorrhage, observation alone leaves embolic risk, and compression of an acutely thrombosed leg is not treatment.
  13. The correct answer is A (The sciatic nerve runs beneath or through piriformis and is compressed by a tight muscle): In piriformis syndrome the sciatic nerve, or its peroneal division, runs beneath or through a tight or hypertrophied piriformis and is compressed, especially in the flexion, adduction and internal rotation position. The tensor fasciae latae and iliotibial band lie lateral and do not contact the sciatic nerve. A normal lumbar MRI and negative straight leg raise point away from a lumbar root source.
  14. The correct answer is C (Coordinate with her prescriber to hold apixaban for the guideline interval, then resume after the procedure): Epidural lead placement is a high bleeding risk procedure because an epidural hematoma can cause cord compression, so the direct oral anticoagulant is held for the recommended interval in coordination with the prescriber. With no prior stroke and a moderate score, a brief interruption without bridging is acceptable. Aspirin does not replace anticoagulation, and reversal agents are for emergencies rather than elective planning.
  15. The correct answer is D (Abnormal temporal dispersion, since the area change is small and the response duration is markedly prolonged): Conduction block requires a substantial loss of area, whereas a large amplitude drop with a small area change and a markedly longer duration indicates that fibers conduct at different speeds, which is temporal dispersion from acquired demyelination. Physiologic dispersion is much smaller than this, and axon loss would lower distal responses too.
  16. The correct answer is D (Return to the prosthetist to revise the socket brim contour and reassess volume-related fit): A soft tissue roll at the proximal socket edge after weight gain signals a socket that is too tight or poorly contoured at the brim. The prosthetist should modify the brim and reassess the fit. Added liner thickness or socks make the socket tighter, and imaging is unnecessary when a mechanical cause is evident.
  17. The correct answer is B (Observe without antibiotics, since treating bacteriuria without symptoms adds resistance but no clinical benefit): In spinal cord injury, bacteriuria without fever, new spasticity, pain, autonomic dysreflexia, or other change is colonization, and guidelines advise against treating it. Antibiotics select resistant organisms and do not reduce later symptomatic infections. Cloudy, malodorous urine and colony count alone do not define infection.
  18. The correct answer is B (Increase the loop diuretic, hold strenuous exercise temporarily, and track daily weights and electrolytes): Weight gain, orthopnea, crackles, and edema signal volume overload in decompensated heart failure, so diuretic intensification with temporary activity reduction is appropriate. Saline worsens congestion. Non-dihydropyridine calcium channel blockers are harmful in reduced ejection fraction, and beta blockers are usually continued unless there is shock or marked bradycardia.
  19. The correct answer is A (A bisphosphonate such as alendronate, which reduces pain in CRPS with high bone turnover findings): Bisphosphonates have randomized trial support for reducing pain in CRPS, particularly when bone scan uptake or osteopenia indicate high turnover. Opioids and NSAIDs lack convincing evidence in this disorder, and lidocaine infusion data are weak and not durable.
  20. The correct answer is C (Increase energy availability through nutrition counseling and a team approach aimed at restoring menses): The female athlete triad is driven by low energy availability, so treatment centers on restoring caloric intake and reducing exercise energy expenditure with a multidisciplinary team. Oral contraceptives do not improve bone density in this setting and can mask the return of natural menses. Bisphosphonates persist in bone and are avoided in young women.
  21. The correct answer is B (Vitamin B12 deficiency from long-term metformin, affecting both posterior columns and peripheral nerves): Chronic metformin use lowers B12 absorption, and B12 deficiency causes a myeloneuropathy with sensory ataxia, absent ankle reflexes from nerve involvement and brisk knee reflexes from cord involvement, plus macrocytosis. Diabetic neuropathy alone would not produce brisk reflexes. Copper deficiency looks similar but usually follows gastric surgery or zinc excess.
  22. The correct answer is A (Flexion enlarges the spinal canal and foramina, easing compression of the cauda equina roots): Neurogenic claudication improves with lumbar flexion because the canal and foramina widen and the ligamentum flavum unbuckles, relieving compression and venous congestion of the roots. Normal pulses and comfortable cycling, which keeps the spine flexed, argue against an arterial cause. Forward bending does not shorten the hamstrings in a way that reduces nerve traction.
  23. The correct answer is A (Greater pinch force without a cable, but higher cost, more weight, and battery needs): Myoelectric hands use surface electrode signals, so no control cable or shoulder harness is needed, and they generate higher pinch force than a typical voluntary-opening hook. The tradeoffs are higher cost, greater weight, battery dependence, and more maintenance. Durability in wet environments and cable-mediated proprioception favor the body-powered hook, which does not fit his stated priorities.
  24. The correct answer is C (Defer the vacuum device until the bone infection is treated with resection and culture-directed antibiotics): Untreated osteomyelitis is a contraindication to negative pressure therapy because the device cannot eradicate infected bone and may delay definitive care. Resection of infected bone and culture-directed antibiotics come first. Lower settings or silver fillers do not change that contraindication.
  25. The correct answer is C (Riluzole modestly extends survival by months, and edaravone slows functional decline in selected early patients): Riluzole extends survival by a few months, and edaravone slowed the decline in function scores in a selected population with early, mild disease. Neither reverses neuron loss or restores strength, and neither delivers a survival gain of years. Supportive care remains essential alongside these agents.
  26. The correct answer is A (The right traversing L5 root, entrapped in the lateral recess by the hypertrophied facet): The lateral recess at L4-L5 contains the traversing L5 root, and great toe extension weakness fits L5. The exiting L4 root lies in the foramen, which is stated to be open. Central canal narrowing is only mild, so a diffuse cauda equina cause is less likely.
  27. The correct answer is C (Hip flexion contracture limiting hip extension, compensated by increased lumbar lordosis during stance): A residual thigh that cannot lie flat on the Thomas test shows a hip flexion contracture, which forces lumbar lordosis and forward lean to keep the center of mass over the prosthesis. Weak extensors can cause a forward lean too, but the positive Thomas test points to a fixed contracture. The socket is stated to fit well.
  28. The correct answer is C (Scheduled acetaminophen within the daily limit, avoiding oral NSAIDs given kidney disease and prior bleeding): Oral NSAIDs, including COX-2 selective agents, reduce prostaglandin-dependent renal perfusion and raise bleeding and blood pressure risk, so they are avoided with stage 4 kidney disease and prior bleeding. Acetaminophen within the daily limit is the safest scheduled choice with normal liver function. A proton pump inhibitor protects the stomach but not the kidney, and tramadol needs renal dose reduction and carries its own risks.
  29. The correct answer is A (Plantar fascia and calf stretching with a supportive heel cushion or orthosis and activity modification): Start-up heel pain with medial tubercle tenderness is plantar fasciitis, and most patients improve with stretching, heel support and activity modification. Spurs are common incidental findings and are not the pain source. Repeated steroid injections risk fat pad atrophy and rupture, and shockwave is reserved for patients who fail initial care.
  30. The correct answer is C (Protein denaturation and axonal degeneration, with gradual partial nerve regeneration over several months): Phenol is a chemical neurolytic that denatures proteins and causes axonal degeneration, so spasticity relief lasts months until the nerve partially regrows. Local anesthetics only block sodium channels transiently. Cleavage of SNAP-25 describes botulinum toxin type A, which acts at the junction rather than on the axon itself.
  31. The correct answer is B (Progressive balance and proprioceptive training on unstable surfaces to restore neuromuscular control of the ankle): Chronic ankle instability with mild laxity and symmetric peroneal strength usually reflects deficits in proprioception and neuromuscular control, so progressive balance training on unstable surfaces is the best addition. Strengthening muscle that is already symmetric adds little, and immobilization worsens deconditioning. Surgery is considered after a failed course of functional rehabilitation.
  32. The correct answer is B (Resting ankle splints holding neutral position, removed regularly for skin checks and range of motion): Sedated patients lie in plantarflexion for hours, so sustained neutral positioning with resting splints, removed for skin inspection and range of motion, is the preventive measure. Brief daily stretching cannot offset the many hours spent shortened. Serial casting treats an established contracture and adds skin risk in a patient who cannot report pressure.
  33. The correct answer is C (Perform a multifactorial fall risk assessment with gait testing, orthostatic blood pressure, and medication review): A positive screen calls for a multifactorial assessment of gait, strength, balance, orthostatic blood pressure, medications, vision, and home hazards, followed by targeted interventions. Her sedative and orthostatic dizziness are modifiable contributors that a single intervention would miss. Vitamin D alone does not reliably lower falls in community dwellers, and bone density estimates fracture risk rather than falls.
  34. The correct answer is B (Test a proximal or facial muscle and hold pyridostigmine for several hours beforehand if clinically safe): In ocular or mild myasthenia gravis the distal hand muscles are often normal, whereas proximal or facial muscles such as nasalis or trapezius have higher yield. Cholinesterase inhibitors can mask a decrement, so holding them before the study improves sensitivity when safe. Warming, not cooling, the limb is standard, since cold reduces decrement.
  35. The correct answer is A (A brief local twitch response of muscle fibers elicited by snapping palpation across the area): The local twitch response, a transient contraction of taut band fibers when the area is snapped, is the most specific physical sign of a trigger point. Creatine kinase and needle electromyography are normal in myofascial pain. Widespread symmetric tender points describe fibromyalgia, not a focal muscle problem.
  36. The correct answer is D (Low muscle strength, such as reduced grip strength or slow repeated chair stands): EWGSOP2 makes low muscle strength the primary parameter, since strength predicts outcomes better than mass. Low strength indicates probable sarcopenia, and low muscle quantity or quality confirms it. Poor physical performance, such as slow gait speed, then marks severe disease.
  37. The correct answer is D (Switch to levetiracetam, which has fewer cerebellar and cognitive effects in brain injury rehabilitation): Cerebellar signs and cognitive slowing on phenytoin in a patient trying to participate in therapy justify changing to a better tolerated agent such as levetiracetam. Phenobarbital and topiramate also impair cognition. A high-normal free level with clear toxicity symptoms should not be dismissed.
  38. The correct answer is B (Valproate titrated to effect with monitoring of hepatic enzymes, platelets, and cognitive side effects): Asthma and a low heart rate make propranolol unsafe, so a mood stabilizing anticonvulsant such as valproate is a reasonable alternative with monitoring of liver function and platelets. Lorazepam and haloperidol can impair recovery and are avoided as routine therapy. Phenytoin does not treat aggression and carries cognitive side effects.
  39. The correct answer is A (Nicotine cessation with vitamin D repletion, since both impair osteoblast function and fracture union): Smoking and vitamin D deficiency are modifiable biological barriers to union, so correcting them is the best addition to a stable construct with a small gap. NSAIDs can impair bone healing. Loading beyond what fixation tolerates risks failure, and revision surgery is reserved for failed biologic correction.
  40. The correct answer is D (About 9, calculated as one divided by the 12 percent absolute risk difference, rounded up): The absolute difference is 30 percent minus 18 percent, or 12 percent, and the number needed to treat is 1 divided by 0.12, about 8.3, rounded up to 9. Using one group's event rate or the relative change gives values that do not describe additional responders per patient treated.
  41. The correct answer is B (Supported employment with on-site job coaching, graded hours, and workplace cognitive accommodations): Supported employment places the person in the real job quickly with a job coach, graded hours, and accommodations, and it has the best evidence after brain injury. Waiting for normal test scores or prolonged clinic based training delays return and generalizes poorly. Unsupported full duty return risks early failure.
  42. The correct answer is D (Observe without antibiotics, since treating asymptomatic bacteriuria promotes resistance and does not prevent symptomatic infection): Chronic catheterization commonly leaves bacteria in the urine without infection, and treating a patient with no symptoms increases resistance without lowering infection risk. Treatment is reserved for symptoms such as fever, new spasticity, autonomic dysreflexia or worsened leakage. Prophylactic antibiotics and irrigation are not recommended.
  43. The correct answer is C (Change to hydromorphone with scheduled acetaminophen, since morphine metabolites accumulate in renal failure): Morphine-3-glucuronide and morphine-6-glucuronide accumulate in advanced kidney disease and cause confusion and myoclonus. Switching to a lower dose of an opioid with less renal dependence, such as hydromorphone, with acetaminophen is appropriate. Meperidine produces a toxic metabolite, and codeine is also unsafe in renal failure.
  44. The correct answer is D (Sleep hygiene and behavioral strategies first, with sertraline added if medication proves clinically necessary): In an older adult with cognitive impairment and a recent fracture, nonpharmacologic measures come first, and an SSRI is the preferred drug if one is needed. Benzodiazepines and diphenhydramine are potentially inappropriate by Beers criteria because of falls, delirium, and anticholinergic effects. Quetiapine carries sedation, orthostasis, and metabolic risk without strong evidence for this use.
  45. The correct answer is D (Postinjection corticosteroid crystal flare, a self-limited reaction that typically resolves within one to two days): Crystalline corticosteroid can cause a transient synovitis within hours of injection that peaks and resolves over one to two days, matching the early onset, absent fever and improvement with ice. Septic arthritis usually declares itself later with fever and worsening pain and would prompt aspiration if the course changed. Hemarthrosis and skin allergy do not fit the picture.
  46. The correct answer is A (Spinal extensor strengthening with postural and balance training while avoiding loaded trunk flexion and twisting): After a stable vertebral fracture, extensor strengthening, posture training, and balance work reduce kyphosis and fall risk while avoiding forces that compress the anterior column. Flexion loading and twisting can cause new fractures. Prolonged rest and high-impact work are both inappropriate in this setting.
  47. The correct answer is C (Hip flexion beyond ninety degrees with adduction and internal rotation of the operated leg): After a posterior approach the posterior capsule is repaired or weakened, and flexion past ninety degrees with adduction and internal rotation levers the head out posteriorly. The opposite combination of extension with external rotation threatens anterior dislocation. Low chairs and commodes are therefore raised with seat risers.
  48. The correct answer is C (Lower phosphate with binders and diet, adding a calcimimetic or vitamin D analog to suppress PTH): The picture is secondary hyperparathyroidism with high-turnover renal osteodystrophy. Control of hyperphosphatemia is the foundation, followed by agents that lower PTH such as calcimimetics or active vitamin D analogs. Aggressive calcium loading worsens vascular calcification, and teriparatide adds PTH activity to an already elevated state.
  49. The correct answer is D (Left trochlear nerve palsy affecting the superior oblique muscle, commonly caused by closed head trauma): Vertical diplopia worse on downgaze, a hypertropic left eye, and head tilt away from the affected side indicate left superior oblique weakness from a trochlear nerve injury, a classic result of closed head trauma. An oculomotor palsy would cause ptosis and pupil involvement, and an abducens palsy causes horizontal diplopia.
  50. The correct answer is C (A moist hot pack over the lateral elbow with towel layers between pack and skin): Cold exposure can trigger vasospasm in Raynaud phenomenon and hemolysis in cold agglutinin disease, so cold and contrast modalities are avoided. Moist heat with a towel barrier is safe with normal sensation and intact skin, and it can precede gentle stretching. A contrast bath still exposes the limb to cold water.
  51. The correct answer is D (Continue conservative care with activity and therapy, since most herniations improve spontaneously over time): She has no progressive motor deficit, her pain is improving, and herniated disc material often regresses, so continued nonoperative care is the best choice. Contact between disc and root on MRI does not by itself indicate surgery. Epidural steroid injection can give short-term relief but has not been shown to reduce eventual surgery, and it is not required when she is already improving.
  52. The correct answer is B (Controls training, practicing opening and closing the terminal device in several arm and body positions): After donning, doffing, and a wearing schedule, the user learns controls training, which means operating the terminal device reliably in different positions, before use training for bimanual and daily tasks. The Glasgow Coma Scale grades acute responsiveness but does not measure memory, and the Rancho scale describes behavior stages without defining when amnesia ends.

Waiting for zero pain delays recovery, and imaging or surgical referral is not indicated for uncomplicated mechanical pain. A graded return is safer than immediate unrestricted lifting.
  216. The correct answer is A (Gastrocnemius recession, which lengthens only the two-joint calf muscle responsible for the knee-dependent contracture): A positive Silfverskiold test, with more dorsiflexion when the knee is flexed, shows that the gastrocnemius alone is short because it crosses the knee. Gastrocnemius recession targets that muscle and spares the soleus, which preserves push off strength. Full Achilles lengthening is reserved for tightness that persists with the knee flexed, and a tendon transfer does not correct equinus.
  217. The correct answer is C (Right facial nerve efferent limb, because the right orbicularis oculi fails to respond to either side): When the muscle on one side cannot respond to stimulation of either side, the facial nerve efferent limb on that side is affected. An afferent lesion would instead abolish responses on both sides when the affected side is stimulated, and the contralateral R2 here is normal after right stimulation. A brainstem interneuron lesion would not selectively remove both responses on one recording side.
  218. The correct answer is D (A centrally penetrant antimuscarinic that blocks cholinergic transmission, adding to anticholinergic burden in dementia): Oxybutynin is a lipophilic antimuscarinic that crosses the blood-brain barrier, worsening cognition and causing constipation, and it is avoided in dementia. A beta-3 agonist acts peripherally and does not carry this risk. Cholinesterase inhibitors increase cholinergic tone and would not explain the confusion with constipation.
  219. The correct answer is C (Acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy, because sural sparing with multifocal block and absent F waves is typical): Prolonged distal latencies, conduction block, temporal dispersion, absent F waves and the sural sparing pattern, in a patient with an antecedent illness and rapid progression, fit acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy. The axonal variants lack block and dispersion, and the chronic form progresses over more than eight weeks.
  220. The correct answer is C (Maximum medical improvement, meaning the condition is stable and unlikely to change with more treatment): Impairment under the AMA Guides is rated only once the patient has reached maximum medical improvement, meaning the condition is stable and unlikely to change substantially with or without further treatment. Persistent pain is compatible with that status, so symptom resolution is not required. Neither full duty nor a fixed elapsed time defines it.
  221. The correct answer is B (Length of posttraumatic amnesia, where durations beyond several weeks predict substantial long-term disability): The duration of posttraumatic amnesia is one of the strongest single predictors of long-term functional and employment outcome, and amnesia lasting many weeks signals substantial disability. The initial Glasgow Coma Scale score is useful but is a weaker predictor than how long the amnesia lasts. A nonsurgical contusion or a reactive pupil does not carry the same prognostic weight.
  222. The correct answer is B (Isotonic saline hydration, then an intravenous bisphosphonate while beginning gradual standing and weight bearing): Immobilization hypercalcemia occurs in young males early after spinal cord injury because bone resorption outpaces formation, with suppressed PTH. Volume repletion with saline comes first, followed by a bisphosphonate and early mobilization. Thiazides reduce urinary calcium loss and would raise the level, and parathyroid surgery treats a different disorder with elevated hormone.
  223. The correct answer is C (Sit the patient upright, loosen tight clothing, and check for bladder distension or catheter blockage): The first steps are to sit the patient up, which lowers pressure by orthostasis, loosen constrictive clothing and look for the trigger, beginning with the bladder. Medication is reserved for persistent hypertension after those steps. Rectal manipulation comes after the bladder and only with topical anesthetic.
  224. The correct answer is D (Catheterize every four hours to keep volumes under about 500 mL, which protects the bladder): Volumes above 500 mL indicate overdistension, which damages the bladder wall and promotes leakage and infection, so the schedule should be tightened. Indwelling catheters increase infection and stone risk. Fluid restriction concentrates the urine, and medications do not fix a volume problem.
  225. The correct answer is B (The palmar cutaneous branch leaves proximally and runs superficial to the flexor retinaculum, outside the tunnel): The palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve arises proximal to the carpal tunnel and crosses over the flexor retinaculum, so it escapes compression. This explains thenar sensory sparing in carpal tunnel syndrome. The recurrent motor branch is a motor nerve and the anterior interosseous nerve carries no skin sensation.
  226. The correct answer is A (Peroneal nerve functional electrical stimulation triggered during swing phase to produce active ankle dorsiflexion): Peroneal nerve stimulation timed to swing phase provides active dorsiflexion without a plastic orthosis, suiting a patient with intact lower motor neurons and orthosis skin problems. Another rigid orthosis repeats the problem he wants to avoid. Toxin into tibialis anterior would weaken an already weak muscle, and tendon transfer is an option only after conservative measures.
  227. The correct answer is C (Asymmetric disc and facet degeneration, managed first with exercise, analgesics and selective epidural injections): Adult degenerative scoliosis arises from asymmetric disc and facet degeneration, producing back pain and neurogenic claudication from lateral recess and foraminal stenosis. First-line care is nonoperative with exercise, analgesics and selective injections. Bracing is not effective in adults, and surgery is reserved for failure of conservative care or neurologic decline.
  228. The correct answer is A (Pain neuroscience education combined with cognitive behavioral therapy and graded activity exposure): Pain neuroscience education reframes pain as a protective system that can become oversensitive, and with cognitive behavioral therapy and graded exposure it reduces fear avoidance and improves function. Passive modalities act on the periphery and give transient relief. Injections and bed rest target a localized generator that this patient does not have.
  229. The correct answer is A (PTSD Checklist for DSM-5 (PCL-5), a self-report measure of trauma-related symptom clusters and severity): The PCL-5 maps to the DSM-5 PTSD symptom clusters of intrusion, avoidance, negative mood and cognition, and arousal, which match his nightmares, hypervigilance, and avoidance. The PHQ-9 and GAD-7 screen depression and generalized anxiety, which may coexist but do not capture trauma-specific symptoms. AUDIT-C is useful in a veteran on opioids but does not address his complaints.
  230. The correct answer is C (Pectoralis major and subscapularis, which hold the shoulder in adduction and internal rotation): The classic hemiplegic posture adducts and internally rotates the shoulder through pectoralis major, subscapularis, and latissimus dorsi. Treating those muscles frees abduction for hygiene and dressing. The elbow and forearm muscles are also overactive but do not restrict axillary access, and the external rotators are weak, not overactive.
  231. The correct answer is A (Bilateral absence occurs with normal aging, so it has little diagnostic weight without other abnormalities): H reflexes frequently become small or absent bilaterally in healthy adults over about 60 years, so bilateral absence is not specific. A unilateral absence or marked side to side asymmetry is much more meaningful. With normal sural and peroneal studies and no symptoms, no disease should be inferred.
  232. The correct answer is C (Video-EEG monitoring that captures a typical event without epileptiform discharges during or after it): Prolonged events with asynchronous movements, eye closure, and preserved recall suggest functional seizures, and the gold standard is recording a typical event on video-EEG with no epileptiform correlate. A repeat routine EEG or MRI can be normal in either condition. Creatine kinase has limited accuracy for separating the two.
  233. The correct answer is B (Spinal cord stimulator, which modulates dorsal column signaling in persistent radicular pain after spine surgery): Spinal cord stimulation is supported for persistent radicular pain after spine surgery when conservative care fails and psychological screening is favorable. An intrathecal baclofen pump treats spasticity, which she does not have. Sacral neuromodulation is used for bladder, bowel and pelvic indications rather than leg pain.
  234. The correct answer is B (Combined upper and lower motor neuron signs with progressive spread, after excluding other disease processes): The El Escorial criteria require progressive spread of upper and lower motor neuron signs across body regions, with electrodiagnostic and clinical evidence and exclusion of mimics. Creatine kinase may be mildly elevated but is not diagnostic. Sensory findings should be normal, and most patients have no family history or identified gene.
  235. The correct answer is C (Focal demyelinating conduction block at the fibular head, which carries a favorable prognosis for recovery): A preserved distal amplitude with a sharp drop across the fibular head and no spontaneous activity indicates conduction block from focal demyelination, usually from compression. Because axons remain intact, recovery is typically good once pressure is removed. Axon loss would lower the distal response and produce fibrillation potentials.
  236. The correct answer is C (Low sensitivity but high specificity for a disc herniation compressing the symptomatic nerve root): The crossed straight leg raise is positive in fewer patients than the ipsilateral test, so it is insensitive, but a positive result is highly specific for a herniated disc affecting the symptomatic root. Because it misses many herniations, a negative result cannot exclude one. Hip pathology does not produce leg pain at a distance from the moved limb in this pattern.
  237. The correct answer is B (Blue, indicating weakly positive birefringence, which identifies calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate crystals): Calcium pyrophosphate dihydrate crystals are rhomboid and weakly positively birefringent, appearing blue when parallel to the compensator axis. Urate crystals are needle shaped, strongly negatively birefringent, and appear yellow when parallel, so mismatched color and crystal pairings fail.
  238. The correct answer is A (Elevated protein with normal cell count, treated with intravenous immunoglobulin or plasma exchange and respiratory monitoring): Ascending weakness, areflexia, and a preceding diarrheal illness suggest Guillain-Barre syndrome, with albuminocytologic dissociation in spinal fluid. Intravenous immunoglobulin or plasma exchange is effective, and falling vital capacity requires close respiratory monitoring. Corticosteroids do not improve outcomes in this disorder.
  239. The correct answer is B (Submaximal strengthening and aerobic training with frequent rest, avoiding overwork and monitoring for symptom worsening): Guillain-Barre recovery is best supported by submaximal strengthening and aerobic exercise with generous rest, advancing slowly while watching for overwork weakness and fatigue. Maximal exertion risks prolonged fatigue and weakness. Bed rest and passive motion alone promote deconditioning and delay recovery.
  240. The correct answer is C (Urgent thoracic spine MRI to evaluate for pathologic fracture and cord compression before any augmentation): Night pain, weight loss, prior breast cancer, and new bilateral leg weakness raise concern for a pathologic fracture with possible spinal cord compression. MRI should be done urgently to define the lesion and the canal. Bracing, augmentation, or bisphosphonates alone would delay recognition of a surgical or oncologic emergency.
  241. The correct answer is A (Latex-free equipment and surroundings for every procedure, since repeated exposure sensitizes children with spina bifida): Children with spina bifida are at high risk for latex sensitization after repeated exposure, and this child has reacted. Complete latex avoidance is the safest strategy, and low-protein gloves still contain latex. Premedication does not reliably prevent anaphylaxis.
  242. The correct answer is A (Short acting oral captopril, which avoids the dangerous interaction with the phosphodiesterase inhibitor): When upright positioning and removal of the trigger fail to lower pressure, a rapid acting agent is used, but nitrates are contraindicated within about 24 hours of sildenafil because of profound hypotension. Captopril avoids that interaction. Beta blockers risk unopposed alpha stimulation and worsen the bradycardia that is typical in this syndrome.
  243. The correct answer is C (Pause and rest, then continue at a lower intensity because the heart rate response is excessive): In early inpatient cardiac rehabilitation the heart rate rise is limited to a modest amount above rest, so a rise of 40 means intensity is too high. The right response is rest and resuming at lower intensity. Age based maximum targets are not used this early, and halting all mobilization is unnecessary.
  244. The correct answer is D (Anteroposterior pelvis radiographs with migration percentage measured at intervals set by GMFCS level): Hip displacement in cerebral palsy is often silent, and its risk rises with GMFCS level, so surveillance uses standardized anteroposterior pelvis radiographs and the migration percentage at set intervals. Clinical range of motion testing alone misses early subluxation. A single film or delayed imaging does not track progression.
  245. The correct answer is C (Relative rest with heel cups and gastrocnemius stretching, allowing sport participation within pain tolerance): Calcaneal apophysitis (Sever disease) is a self-limited traction overuse injury, and sclerosis with fragmentation is a normal radiographic variant. Treatment is relative rest, heel cups, calf stretching and activity modification. Casting is reserved for severe cases, and injection or surgery has no role.
  246. The correct answer is B (Left hand, moving it with the painful right leg to offload the right hip): A cane held in the hand opposite the painful hip widens the base of support and lets the cane share load as the painful limb is in stance. It advances with the painful limb so both support the body together. Using it on the same side or with the opposite leg removes this load-sharing effect.
  247. The correct answer is A (Mirror therapy with graded motor imagery, aimed at correcting maladaptive cortical reorganization and sensory mismatch): Mirror therapy and graded motor imagery target the cortical reorganization thought to sustain phantom limb pain and need no medication. Revision surgery does not treat phantom pain when no neuroma is present, and long-acting opioid monotherapy is not recommended as first-line for chronic neuropathic pain. Sympathetic blocks are reserved for selected, sympathetically maintained pain.
  248. The correct answer is D (Estimate creatinine clearance, since low muscle mass makes serum creatinine overestimate kidney function): In a small, frail older woman, low muscle mass keeps serum creatinine falsely normal, and a Cockcroft-Gault estimate here gives a creatinine clearance near 30 mL/min. Gabapentin accumulates when clearance is reduced, so the dose should follow the estimated clearance. A fixed halving rule ignores individual renal function, and waiting for toxicity is unsafe.
  249. The correct answer is A (Gastrocnemius tightness, because it crosses the knee and loses length when the knee is extended): A Silfverskiold test showing more dorsiflexion with the knee flexed than extended localizes the shortening to the gastrocnemius, which crosses the knee, and this guides selective lengthening or toxin injection. If the soleus were the cause, range would be equally limited at both knee positions.
  250. The correct answer is D (Radial tunnel syndrome from posterior interosseous nerve compression at the supinator arcade): Pain distal to the epicondyle over the supinator, night symptoms and pain with resisted supination suggest radial tunnel syndrome. Lateral epicondylopathy is tender at the epicondyle itself and with resisted wrist extension. Cervical radiculopathy would show neck pain, reflex or sensory changes.
